1	# CLAUDE.md
2	
3	This file provides guidance to Claude Code (claude.ai/code) when working with code in this repository.
4	
5	## Mandatory instructions
6	
7	- Log all prompts to docs/prompts.md, including a one-line summary as the header, the date & time of the prompt, and the text of the prompt verbatim.
8	- After each task, commit the current working tree to git. Only commit,
9	  never push.
10	
11	# IRC Bot — Claude Code Instructions
12	
13	## Project overview
14	
15	This project is a C# IRC bot called Marv targetting .NET 10.
16	
17	The goal of this project is to provide a robust IRC bot capable of interfacing
18	with all major IRC servers/networks, and utilizing IRCv3 features to create a
19	seamless experience for developers and users.
20	
21	The bot will only support connection to a single IRC network at a time, and is
22	only concerned with real-time communications; no effort will be taken to handle
23	historic chat messages through features like PLAYBACK, nor will features such
24	as DCC be supported.
25	
26	## Design goals
27	
28	- Maintenance - Assume that a human will be maintaining the project without
29	  AI assistance. Comments, documentation and tests should be robust and clear.
30	- Modularity - While the core of Marv will implement some basic logic, setup
31	  and common functionality, most functionality will be implemented as plugins.
32	- Developer Experience (DX) - Because most functionality will live in plugins,
33	  DX is a priority; care should be taken to make plugin authoring as simple as
34	  possible and commonly used code should be provided by the core component.
35	  Debugging and trace logging are also a key component of DX.
36	
37	## Specific architecture goals
38	
39	- There will be a command-line application which serves as the user interface
40	  to Marv. This will read the config, initialize logging, validate and load
41	  plugins and run the bot's main loop.
42	  In addition to file-based configuration, the app will support configuration
43	  by environment variable and command-line arguments.
44	- There will be a core assembly containing the bot's core logic (connecting to
45	  the IRC server, negotiating capabilities, authenticating to IRC services,
46	  managing oper state and joining initial channels) and plugin management.
47	- Plugins will be able to provide services to each other through a service
48	  registry managed by the core assembly. The interfaces defining these services
49	  may be implemented in any loaded assembly, including the plugin's assembly or 
50	  a separate contracts assembly.
51	- Plugins will be able to respond to all aspects of the bot's lifecycle, the
52	  connection's lifecycle, and events which occur on the IRC server. They will
53	  be able to opt-in to responding to individual protocol messages, but there
54	  should also be DX-friendly abstractions provided by the plugin base class and
55	  the bot class so that this is rarely necessary.
56	- There should be multiple example plugins provided to demonstrate best
57	  practices of plugin authoring, as well as to test the plugin interfaces.
58	
59	## Non-negotiable rules
60	
61	- All code must compile before presenting results. Run `dotnet build`
62	  and fix all errors.
63	- All tests must pass before presenting results. Run `dotnet test`
64	  and fix all failures.
65	- Never modify the plugin API surface without explicitly flagging it
66	  and explaining why.
67	- Prefer `async`/`await` throughout. Use `System.Threading.Channels`
68	  for internal message passing.
69	
70	## Coding conventions
71	
72	- C# 13 / .NET 10 features are encouraged where they aid clarity
73	- Nullable reference types enabled everywhere
74	- One class per file, filename matches class name
75	- XML doc comments on all public API members
76	
77	## Architecture decisions
78	
79	Stored in `/docs/adr/`. Read relevant ADRs before making any
80	structural changes.
81	
82	## How to run things
83	
84	When running in a dev container, there is an IRC server available on localhost.
85	This IRC server can be started and stopped with `sudo service ngircd start` and
86	`sudo service ngircd stop` respectively.
87	
88	To simplify building and testing the bot, provide a makefile that builds and
89	tests the bot, and copies the bot and all plugins into a single directory for
90	the user to run.

Tool reply2026-05-30T14:45:19.183Z
1	# Architecture
2	
3	This document describes Marv's assembly structure, internal layering,
4	async/threading model, plugin loading, and inter-plugin service system.
5	
6	---
7	
8	## Assembly Structure
9	
10	```
11	Marv.sln
12	├── src/
13	│   ├── Marv.App/            # CLI host application
14	│   ├── Marv.Core/           # Core library
15	│   └── plugins/
16	│       ├── Marv.Plugins.Ping/       # Example: simple ping/pong
17	│       ├── Marv.Plugins.Auth/       # Example: auth service provider
18	│       └── Marv.Plugins.Greet/      # Example: consumes auth service
19	├── tests/
20	│   ├── Marv.Core.Tests/
21	│   └── Marv.Plugins.Tests/
22	└── docs/
23	```
24	
25	### Marv.App
26	
27	The CLI host application. A thin shell that delegates to the core.
28	
29	Responsibilities:
30	
31	- Parse command-line arguments (using `System.CommandLine` 2.x)
32	- Read configuration from files, environment variables, and CLI args
33	  (in ascending priority order)
34	- Initialize logging (`Microsoft.Extensions.Logging` with console and
35	  file sinks)
36	- Call `services.AddMarv(configuration)` to delegate plugin loading,
37	  dependency sorting, service registration, and bot setup to the core
38	- Run the hosted service and handle graceful shutdown (Ctrl+C /
39	  SIGTERM)
40	
41	The app does **not** perform plugin discovery, DI wiring, or bot
42	orchestration itself — that is the core's responsibility. The app
43	builds the `IHostBuilder`, registers configuration and logging, calls
44	the core's extension method, and runs.
45	
46	This assembly references `Marv.Core` and the .NET hosting/DI packages.
47	It does not reference any plugin assemblies directly — plugins are
48	loaded at runtime.
49	
50	### Marv.Core
51	
52	The core library. Contains everything a plugin author needs to
53	reference at compile time, and everything the host needs to run the
54	bot. This is the only assembly that plugin projects reference.
55	
56	Responsibilities:
57	
58	- IRC message parsing and serialization
59	- TCP/TLS connection management
60	- IRCv3 capability negotiation and SASL authentication
61	- Protocol-level message handling (PING/PONG, CTCP VERSION/PING/TIME)
62	- Rate-limited message sending
63	- Channel/user/mode state tracking
64	- The `IPlugin` interface and `MarvPlugin` convenience base class
65	- Plugin attributes and lifecycle management
66	- Event types and the event dispatch system
67	- The `IBot` facade interface
68	- Plugin discovery, dependency sorting, and lifecycle management
69	- The `IServiceCollection` extension method (`AddMarv`) that wires
70	  everything together
71	
72	### Plugin Assemblies
73	
74	Each plugin is a separate assembly (class library) that references
75	`Marv.Core`. A plugin assembly contains:
76	
77	- Exactly one plugin class implementing `IPlugin` (typically via
78	  `MarvPlugin`)
79	- Optional configuration record/class tagged with
80	  `[PluginConfig(Section = "Name")]`
81	- Optional service interfaces (or these may live in a separate
82	  contracts assembly shared between provider and consumer)
83	- Optional service implementations
84	- Optional handler group classes
85	
86	Plugin assemblies are built as DLLs and placed in a known directory.
87	The host discovers them at startup via the configured plugin paths.
88	
89	---
90	
91	## Internal Layering (Marv.Core)
92	
93	The core library is organized into layers, each building on the one
94	below it. Higher layers depend on lower layers; lower layers never
95	reference higher layers.
96	
97	```
98	┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
99	│            Plugin System                │  Plugin loading, lifecycle,
100	│   IPlugin, MarvPlugin, attributes       │  event dispatch
101	├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
102	│            Bot / Message Processor      │  IBot — high-level API
103	│   Send messages, query state,           │  exposed to plugins.
104	│   PING/PONG, CAP, state updates,        │  Protocol handling and
105	│   event fan-out to plugin tasks         │  state management.
106	├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
107	│            State Tracking               │  Channels, users, modes,
108	│   IChannelStore, IUserStore             │  ISUPPORT parameters
109	├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
110	│            Connection                   │  TCP/TLS, reconnection,
111	│   IIrcConnection                        │  rate limiting
112	├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
113	│            Protocol                     │  IrcMessage parsing,
114	│   IrcParser, IrcSerializer              │  serialization, tags,
115	│                                         │  case mapping
116	└─────────────────────────────────────────┘
117	```
118	
119	### Protocol Layer
120	
121	- `IrcMessage`: Immutable record representing a parsed IRC message
122	  (tags, source, command, parameters). Used for both inbound and
123	  outbound messages — the structure is identical (tags, command,
124	  parameters), with the only difference being that inbound messages
125	  have a source prefix and outbound messages do not. Keeping a single
126	  type avoids conversion overhead and lets plugins inspect/transform
127	  messages uniformly.
128	- `IrcParser`: Parses raw bytes/strings into `IrcMessage` instances.
129	  Handles tag value escaping, multi-space separation, empty trailing
130	  parameters, and source prefix parsing. Validated against
131	  ircdocs/parser-tests vectors.
132	- `IrcSerializer`: Serializes `IrcMessage` back to wire format.
133	- `CaseMapping`: Implements RFC 1459, strict-RFC 1459, and ASCII case
134	  mapping for nick/channel comparison. The active mapping is read from
135	  ISUPPORT and can change at runtime.
136	
137	### Connection Layer
138	
139	- `IIrcConnection`: Abstracts the TCP/TLS connection. Provides a
140	  `ChannelReader<IrcMessage>` for inbound messages and accepts
141	  outbound messages via a `ChannelWriter<IrcMessage>`.
142	- Implements rate limiting on outbound messages using a token bucket
143	  algorithm to prevent excess flood disconnections.
144	- Handles reconnection with exponential backoff.
145	- UTF-8 encoding with graceful handling of invalid sequences.
146	
147	The connection layer is a pure transport — it does not interpret
148	message content. PING/PONG handling, CAP negotiation, and all other
149	protocol-level logic live in the message processor (bot layer) where
150	they have access to state and can be tested without a network
151	connection.
152	
153	### State Tracking
154	
155	- `IChannelStore`: Maintains the set of channels the bot is in, each
156	  with its topic, modes, and member list (including per-user prefixes
157	  and join times).
158	- `IUserStore`: Maintains known users (nick, user, host, account, away
159	  status). Updated from NAMES, WHO, JOIN, PART, QUIT, NICK, CHGHOST,
160	  ACCOUNT, AWAY, SETNAME messages.
161	- `IModeParser`: Parses MODE changes using the server's CHANMODES and
162	  PREFIX from ISUPPORT to correctly handle type A/B/C/D modes.
163	- All comparisons use the server's advertised CASEMAPPING.
164	
165	State stores are written by the message processor task and read by
166	plugin tasks. `IUser` and `IChannel` objects are mutable — the
167	message processor updates properties in place. Only the message
168	processor writes, so no write contention exists.
169	
170	Thread safety for concurrent reads is achieved through:
171	
172	- Atomic individual property reads (reference type fields in .NET)
173	- `ConcurrentDictionary` for collection-valued properties, ensuring
174	  safe enumeration while the message processor adds/removes entries
175	
176	Cross-property consistency within a single `IUser` or `IChannel` is
177	not guaranteed during a handler — a state change could land between
178	two reads. This is rare in practice, and plugins needing strict
179	consistency can copy values into locals at handler entry. Plugins
180	holding a reference to an `IUser` or `IChannel` see live updates
181	to that object's properties.
182	
183	### Bot / Message Processor
184	
185	- `IBot`: The primary interface plugins interact with. Provides
186	  methods for sending messages, querying channel/user state, and
187	  checking capability availability. See `plugin-api-draft.md` for the
188	  full surface.
189	- The message processor is the central task that reads from the
190	  inbound channel and:
191	  - Handles PING/PONG (responds immediately, not exposed to plugins)
192	  - Handles core CTCP queries (VERSION, PING, TIME) — responds
193	    automatically without exposing host information; only the bot
194	    version string is included. Other CTCP queries are translated
195	    into `CtcpEvent` for plugin handling.
196	  - Drives CAP negotiation and SASL authentication
197	  - Updates state tracking (channels, users, modes)
198	  - Translates raw `IrcMessage` into typed events
199	  - Fans out events to each plugin's individual event channel
200	
201	### Plugin System
202	
203	- Plugin discovery, assembly loading, dependency graph construction,
204	  topological sorting, and lifecycle management.
205	- Per-plugin event channels and tasks (see Async/Threading Model).
206	- Described in detail below.
207	
208	---
209	
210	## Async / Threading Model
211	
212	Marv uses a small number of long-lived async tasks communicating via
213	`System.Threading.Channels`. Each plugin runs on its own dedicated
214	task, receiving events through its own channel.
215	
216	### Task Structure
217	
218	```
219	                                             ┌──────────────────┐
220	                                          ┌─►│  Plugin A Task   │
221	                                          │  │  Channel<Event>  │
222	┌──────────────┐   Channel<IrcMessage>   ┌┴───────────────────┐ │
223	│  Read Loop   │ ──────────────────────► │  Message           │ │
224	│  (network)   │   (inbound)             │  Processor         │ │
225	└──────────────┘                         │                    ├─┤
226	                                         │  - PING/PONG       │ │
227	                                         │  - CAP negotiation │ │  ┌──────────────────┐
228	                                         │  - State updates   ├─┼─►│  Plugin B Task   │
229	                                         │  - Event fan-out   │ │  │  Channel<Event>  │
230	                                         └┬───────────────────┘ │  └──────────────────┘
231	                                          │                     │
232	                                          └─►  ... more plugins
233	
234	         (plugins call IBot.SendAsync from any task)
235	                          │
236	                          ▼
237	┌──────────────┐   Channel<IrcMessage>   ┌──────────────────┐
238	│  Write Loop  │ ◄────────────────────── │  Rate Limiter    │
239	│  (network)   │   (outbound)            │  (token bucket)  │
240	└──────────────┘                         └──────────────────┘
241	```
242	
243	1. **Read Loop**: A dedicated async task reads from the TCP stream,
244	   parses raw lines into `IrcMessage` instances, and writes them to
245	   the inbound channel. This task does no processing beyond parsing.
246	
247	2. **Message Processor**: A dedicated async task reads from the
248	   inbound channel. For each message it:
249	   - Handles protocol-level concerns (PING/PONG, CAP negotiation)
250	   - Updates state tracking (channels, users, modes)
251	   - Translates raw `IrcMessage` into typed events
252	   - Writes the event to each plugin's individual event channel
253	
254	3. **Plugin Tasks**: Each plugin has its own dedicated async task and
255	   `Channel<MarvEvent>`. The task reads events from the channel and
256	   calls `plugin.HandleEventAsync(event, ct)` for each one — the
257	   core never calls handler methods directly. This means:
258	   - Each plugin processes events independently and concurrently with
259	     other plugins
260	   - Within a single plugin, `HandleEventAsync` is never called
261	     concurrently with itself — no concurrency within a plugin
262	   - A slow plugin does not block other plugins or state tracking
263	   - Event ordering is preserved within each plugin
264	
265	4. **Rate Limiter**: Accepts outbound messages from any task (plugins
266	   call `IBot.SendAsync` which writes to this component) and drains
267	   them to the outbound channel at a rate that respects the server's
268	   flood limits.
269	
270	5. **Write Loop**: A dedicated async task reads from the outbound
271	   channel and writes serialized messages to the TCP stream.
272	
273	### Concurrency Rules
274	
275	- **`HandleEventAsync` is called sequentially within each plugin task.**
276	  A plugin's event handler is never called concurrently with itself.
277	  But different plugins' handlers do run concurrently.
278	
279	- **State stores are read-safe from any plugin task.** The message
280	  processor updates state before fanning out events. State stores use
281	  concurrent-read-safe data structures, so plugins can safely query
282	  `IBot.Channels` and `IBot.Users` from their event handlers without
283	  synchronization.
284	
285	- **`IBot.SendAsync` (and its variants) is thread-safe.** It can be
286	  called from any plugin task, background tasks, timers, etc.
287	
288	- **`CancellationToken` propagation**: All async methods accept a
289	  `CancellationToken`. The bot's top-level token is cancelled on
290	  shutdown, which drains the channels and allows tasks to exit cleanly.
291	
292	### Labeled Response Correlation
293	
294	When the bot sends a command that expects a response (e.g., WHO,
295	WHOIS), it uses the `labeled-response` capability to tag the outbound
296	message with a unique label. The message processor collects responses
297	with matching labels and delivers them as a batch to the caller via a
298	`TaskCompletionSource<T>`. This allows:
299	
300	```
301	var whoReply = await bot.SendAndAwaitAsync(whoMessage, cancellationToken);
302	```
303	
304	`SendAndAwaitAsync` sends an IRC command and returns the server's
305	correlated response messages. It uses the `labeled-response` IRCv3
306	capability to tag the outbound message with a unique label, then
307	collects all inbound messages bearing that label until the server
308	signals completion. This is useful for commands like WHO, WHOIS, and
309	LIST where the response spans multiple messages.
310	
311	If `labeled-response` is not negotiated, the bot falls back to
312	sequential command queuing with timeout-based correlation.
313	
314	---
315	
316	## Plugin Discovery and Loading
317	
318	### Startup Sequence
319	
320	Plugin loading happens during application startup, before the IRC
321	connection is established. The app calls
322	`services.AddMarv(configuration)`, which triggers the following
323	sequence inside `Marv.Core`.
324	
325	#### Phase 1: Bootstrap (No DI)
326	
327	1. **Read plugin paths**: From the configuration (already parsed by
328	   the app), extract the list of plugin assembly paths.
329	
330	2. **Discover assemblies**: For each configured plugin path, load the
331	   assembly into an `AssemblyLoadContext`. Scan for types that
332	   implement `IPlugin` — each assembly must contain exactly one. Also
333	   scan for configuration classes tagged with `[PluginConfig]` and
334	   handler group classes tagged with `[HandlerGroup]`. Read each
335	   plugin's `static abstract PluginName` property (from the `IPlugin`
336	   interface) for identification in logs, config, and diagnostics.
337	
338	3. **Build dependency graph**: Inspect each discovered plugin type:
339	   - Read `[ProvidesService]` attributes for service types the plugin
340	     provides
341	   - Read `[DependsOn]` attributes for explicit plugin ordering
342	   - Read constructor parameters to identify service dependencies
343	     (non-core types). Non-nullable parameters are required;
344	     nullable parameters with a default of `null` are optional.
345	   Construct a directed graph of plugin dependencies.
346	
347	4. **Topological sort**: Sort the graph. If there is a cycle, report
348	   it and fail startup. If a required dependency is missing, report
349	   it and fail startup. Optional dependencies participate in ordering
350	   (if present, the provider loads first) but do not block startup if
351	   absent.
352	
353	#### Phase 2: DI Container Build
354	
355	5. **Core service registration**: Register core services into the
356	   `IServiceCollection`: logging, configuration, `IIrcConnection`,
357	   `ICapabilityManager`, `IChannelStore`, `IUserStore`, `IBot`.
358	
359	6. **Plugin configuration registration**: For each configuration class
360	   tagged with `[PluginConfig(Section = "Name")]`, automatically
361	   register `IOptions<TConfig>` bound to the `Plugins:{Section}`
362	   configuration section. No boilerplate needed in the plugin —
363	   plugins access configuration via constructor injection of
364	   `IOptions<TConfig>`.
365	
366	7. **Plugin service registration**: For each plugin type (in
367	   dependency order), call its static `ConfigureServices` method
368	   (defined on `IPlugin` with an empty default implementation).
369	   Plugins that provide services to other plugins register their
370	   implementations here. Plugins that only handle events and use
371	   configuration do not need to override this method.
372	
373	8. **Build container**: Call `BuildServiceProvider()` to create the
374	   single `IServiceProvider`. Note that plugin types and handler
375	   group types are **not** registered in the container — they are
376	   created via `ActivatorUtilities` (through `IPluginActivator`).
377	
378	9. **Instantiate plugins**: Create each plugin instance via
379	   `ActivatorUtilities.CreateInstance`. Constructor parameters
380	   (configuration, services, `IBot`, `IPluginActivator`) are
381	   resolved from the container. Handler groups are created by
382	   `MarvPlugin` via `IPluginActivator` during construction.
383	
384	10. **Initialize plugins**: Call `OnLoadAsync()` on each plugin in
385	    dependency order. `MarvPlugin`'s default implementation forwards
386	    to handler group lifecycle methods.
387	
388	#### Runtime
389	
390	12. **Connect**: Establish the IRC connection, negotiate capabilities,
391	    authenticate, join channels.
392	
393	13. **Notify plugins**: Call `OnConnectedAsync()` on each plugin and
394	    its handler groups.
395	
396	14. **Start plugin tasks**: Create a dedicated `Channel<MarvEvent>`
397	    and async task for each plugin. The task reads events and calls
398	    `plugin.HandleEventAsync(event, ct)` — the core never calls
399	    handler methods directly.
400	
401	    For `MarvPlugin` subclasses, `HandleEventAsync` uses reflection
402	    to dispatch to attributed handler methods (`[OnEvent]`,
403	    `[OnCommand]`, `[OnRegex]`, `[OnRawMessage]`, `[OnInterval]`)
404	    on both the plugin class and its handler groups. The core does
405	    not interact with handler groups at all — `MarvPlugin` owns
406	    their creation (via `IPluginActivator`), lifecycle forwarding,
407	    and event dispatch. Handler methods on the plugin class do not
408	    need to be public (dispatch is from within the base class);
409	    handler methods on handler groups must be public. If multiple
410	    handlers match the same event, they are called consecutively in
411	    an undefined order.
412	
413	    Direct `IPlugin` implementations provide their own
414	    `HandleEventAsync` logic.
415	
416	15. **Message loop**: Process messages, update state, fan out events
417	    to plugin channels.
418	
419	16. **Reconnection**: On disconnection, all state is discarded:
420	    pending `SendAndAwaitAsync` calls are cancelled, outbound message
421	    queues are cleared, and channel/user state stores are reset.
422	    Plugins are notified via `OnDisconnectedAsync`, then
423	    `OnUnloadAsync`. Plugin and handler group instances are discarded.
424	    The bot reconnects with exponential backoff, then reinstantiates
425	    all plugins via `ActivatorUtilities` (step 9) and repeats from
426	    step 10. This ensures no stale references survive a reconnection.
427	
428	17. **Shutdown**: Signal all plugin tasks to stop. Call
429	    `OnDisconnectedAsync()` then `OnUnloadAsync()` on each plugin
430	    (and its handler groups) in reverse dependency order. Dispose the
431	    DI container.
432	
433	### Assembly Load Context
434	
435	Each plugin assembly is loaded into a shared `AssemblyLoadContext`.
436	Using a single shared context (rather than isolated contexts per
437	plugin) avoids type identity issues — interfaces defined in
438	`Marv.Core` or in a shared contracts assembly are the same `Type`
439	across all plugins.
440	
441	If we later need assembly isolation (e.g., for hot-reloading or
442	conflicting dependencies), we can revisit this, but the single-context
443	approach is simpler and avoids a class of subtle bugs.
444	
445	---
446	
447	## Inter-Plugin Services
448	
449	### How It Works
450	
451	**Providing a service**: A plugin declares `[ProvidesService]` on its
452	class and overrides the static `ConfigureServices` method from
453	`IPlugin`. The attribute tells the dependency sorter which plugin
454	provides which service type; the method does the actual DI
455	registration.
456	
457	**Consuming a service**: A plugin declares a constructor parameter of
458	the service type. The plugin loader inspects constructor parameters to
459	identify consumed services. Non-nullable parameters are required
460	dependencies; nullable parameters with a default of `null` are
461	optional.
462	
463	**Explicit ordering**: `[DependsOn(typeof(OtherPlugin))]` forces load
464	ordering without implying a service relationship.
465	
466	### Example
467	
468	```csharp
469	// Auth plugin provides IAuthorizationService
470	[ProvidesService(typeof(IAuthorizationService))]
471	public class AuthPlugin : MarvPlugin
472	{
473	    public static string PluginName => "Auth";
474	    public AuthPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator)
475	        : base(bot, activator) { }
476	
477	    public static void ConfigureServices(IServiceCollection services)
478	    {
479	        services.AddSingleton<IAuthorizationService, AccountBasedAuthService>();
480	    }
481	}
482	
483	// Moderation plugin consumes IAuthorizationService (required)
484	public class ModerationPlugin : MarvPlugin
485	{
486	    public static string PluginName => "Moderation";
487	    public ModerationPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator,
488	        IAuthorizationService auth) : base(bot, activator) { ... }
489	}
490	
491	// Greet plugin consumes IAuthorizationService (optional)
492	public class GreetPlugin : MarvPlugin
493	{
494	    public static string PluginName => "Greet";
495	    public GreetPlugin(
496	        IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator,
497	        IOptions<GreetPluginConfig> config,
498	        IAuthorizationService? auth = null) : base(bot, activator) { ... }
499	}
500	```
501	
502	The loader sees:
503	- `AuthPlugin` provides `IAuthorizationService` (from attribute)
504	- `ModerationPlugin` requires `IAuthorizationService` (from
505	  constructor, non-nullable)
506	- `GreetPlugin` optionally uses `IAuthorizationService` (from
507	  constructor, nullable with default `null`)
508	
509	Load order: AuthPlugin → ModerationPlugin, GreetPlugin (order between
510	Moderation and Greet is unspecified since neither depends on the
511	other).
512	
513	### Load Order Details
514	
515	The dependency sorter builds a graph from:
516	
517	- `[DependsOn(typeof(OtherPlugin))]` — direct plugin dependency
518	- Required constructor parameters — resolved to the plugin with a
519	  matching `[ProvidesService]` attribute
520	- Nullable constructor parameters with default `null` —
521	  ordered-if-present
522	
523	The graph is topologically sorted. Plugins with no dependencies load
524	first; plugins with dependencies load after their providers.
525	
526	**Cycle detection**: A cycle in the dependency graph is a
527	configuration error. The bot reports the cycle and refuses to start.
528	
529	**Missing required dependency**: If plugin A requires
530	`IAuthorizationService` but no loaded plugin provides it, startup
531	fails with a clear error message naming the missing service and the
532	plugin(s) that need it.
533	
534	**Missing optional dependency**: If plugin A optionally consumes
535	`IAuthorizationService` and no plugin provides it, A loads normally.
536	The constructor receives `null` for that parameter.
537	
538	### Diagnostics
539	
540	At startup (and available via a status command), the bot logs:
541	
542	- Which plugins are loaded, in what order
543	- Which services each plugin provides (from `[ProvidesService]`)
544	- Which services each plugin consumes (from constructors)
545	- Any plugins that were skipped and why
546	
547	---
548	
549	## Configuration
550	
551	Configuration is layered, with later sources overriding earlier ones:
552	
553	1. **Default values** (hardcoded in configuration classes)
554	2. **Configuration file** (`marv.json` by default). A `--config`
555	   CLI argument allows specifying an alternative path; the file
556	   extension determines the format (`.json` for JSON, `.yaml`/`.yml`
557	   for YAML, etc. via the appropriate
558	   `Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration` provider).
559	3. **Environment variables** (`MARV_*` prefix, double-underscore for
560	   nesting: `MARV_IRC__SERVER`)
561	4. **Command-line arguments** (`--irc:server=irc.example.com`)
562	
563	This uses the standard `Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration` stack.
564	Command-line parsing uses `System.CommandLine` 2.x.
565	
566	### Plugin Configuration
567	
568	Plugins that need configuration declare a configuration class tagged
569	with `[PluginConfig]`:
570	
571	```csharp
572	[PluginConfig(Section = "Greet")]
573	public record GreetPluginConfig
574	{
575	    public string GreetMessage { get; init; } = "Hello, {nick}!";
576	    public bool GreetOnJoin { get; init; } = true;
577	}
578	
579	public class GreetPlugin : MarvPlugin
580	{
581	    public static string PluginName => "Greet";
582	
583	    private readonly GreetPluginConfig _config;
584	
585	    public GreetPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator,
586	        IOptions<GreetPluginConfig> config) : base(bot, activator)
587	    {
588	        _config = config.Value;
589	    }
590	}
591	```
592	
593	The plugin loader discovers configuration classes tagged with
594	`[PluginConfig(Section = "Name")]` during assembly scanning and
595	automatically registers `IOptions<TConfig>` bound to the
596	`Plugins:{Section}` configuration section. Plugins access their
597	configuration via constructor injection of `IOptions<TConfig>`.
598	
599	There is no need for a `ConfigureServices` method just for
600	configuration — this is handled automatically. Only plugins that
601	register services for other plugins to consume need
602	`ConfigureServices`.
603	
604	---
605	
606	## Error Handling
607	
608	- **Parse errors**: Malformed messages from the server are logged and
609	  skipped — they never crash the message processor.
610	- **Plugin handler exceptions**: Caught by the plugin's event loop
611	  task, logged with the plugin name and event type, and do not affect
612	  other plugins or the message loop.
613	- **Connection loss**: The connection layer signals disconnection. All
614	  pending `SendAndAwaitAsync` calls are cancelled, outbound message
615	  queues are cleared, and channel/user state stores are reset. The bot
616	  notifies plugins via `OnDisconnectedAsync`, then begins reconnection
617	  with exponential backoff.
618	- **Startup failures**: Missing required plugins/services, config
619	  validation errors, and dependency cycles all produce clear error
620	  messages and prevent the bot from starting.
621

1	# Platform Abstraction Draft
2	
3	This document describes the core concepts that Marv models and
4	presents to plugins. Although Marv only targets IRC, these
5	abstractions exist to give plugins a clean, typed API rather than
6	forcing them to work with raw protocol strings.
7	
8	---
9	
10	## Messages
11	
12	### Raw Message: `IrcMessage`
13	
14	The lowest-level representation. An immutable record used for both
15	inbound and outbound messages:
16	
17	```
18	IrcMessage
19	├── Tags: IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string?>
20	├── Source: MessageSource?  (nick, user, host — any component may be absent)
21	├── Command: string         ("PRIVMSG", "001", "CAP", etc.)
22	└── Parameters: IReadOnlyList<string>
23	```
24	
25	**Design notes**:
26	
27	- The trailing parameter (after `:`) is always folded into
28	  `Parameters` as the last element. There is no separate `Trailing`
29	  property — this avoids the class of bugs described in research
30	  section 5.1.
31	- `Command` is always uppercase for consistency, regardless of how the
32	  server sent it.
33	- `Tags` values are already unescaped. The parser handles `\:`, `\s`,
34	  `\\`, `\r`, `\n`, and invalid escape sequences per the IRCv3 spec.
35	- `Source` is parsed into its components. Server-originated messages
36	  may have a source with only a hostname (no nick/user). For outbound
37	  messages, `Source` is null.
38	- The same type is used for both directions. The structure is
39	  identical; only the presence of `Source` differs.
40	
41	### Message Metadata
42	
43	Common tags are surfaced as strongly-typed properties on event objects
44	so plugins don't need to dig into the tag dictionary:
45	
46	| Property | Tag | Type | Availability |
47	|---|---|---|---|
48	| `Timestamp` | `time` | `DateTimeOffset` | When `server-time` is negotiated |
49	| `MessageId` | `msgid` | `string?` | When server assigns one |
50	| `Account` | `account` | `string?` | When `account-tag` is negotiated |
51	| `IsBot` | `bot` | `bool` | When `bot` tag is present |
52	| `Label` | `label` | `string?` | Internal use for labeled-response |
53	| `BatchId` | `batch` | `string?` | When message is part of a batch |
54	
55	Plugins can always access the full tag dictionary via `RawMessage.Tags`
56	for non-standard or vendor-specific tags.
57	
58	### High-Level Message Types
59	
60	Plugins typically work with typed event objects rather than raw
61	`IrcMessage`. See the Events section below. The event objects contain
62	the relevant `IrcMessage` for plugins that need protocol-level access.
63	
64	---
65	
66	## Channels
67	
68	### `IChannel`
69	
70	Represents a channel the bot is currently a member of.
71	
72	```
73	IChannel
74	├── Name: string                            ("#channel")
75	├── Topic: string?
76	├── TopicSetBy: string?                     (nick or mask)
77	├── TopicSetAt: DateTimeOffset?
78	├── Modes: IReadOnlyDictionary<char, string?> (mode → parameter)
79	├── Members: IReadOnlyCollection<IUser>
80	├── GetPrefixes(string nick): IReadOnlySet<char>   ('@', '+', etc.)
81	├── GetJoinTime(string nick): DateTimeOffset?
82	├── HasMember(string nick): bool
83	├── IsOp(string nick): bool                (shorthand: has '@' prefix)
84	├── IsVoiced(string nick): bool             (shorthand: has '+' prefix)
85	└── CreatedAt: DateTimeOffset?
86	```
87	
88	Per-user channel state (prefixes and join time) is stored on the
89	channel itself rather than in a separate `IChannelMember` relation.
90	These are the only two per-user-per-channel properties, and a
91	dedicated join type would add API surface without meaningful benefit.
92	Plugins query prefix/join state through the `IChannel` methods.
93	
94	### Channel Comparisons
95	
96	Channel names are compared using the server's advertised `CASEMAPPING`
97	(from ISUPPORT). The `IChannel` and related types use this
98	automatically — plugins do not need to handle case mapping themselves.
99	
100	All channel collections are keyed using case-mapped comparison so that
101	`#Channel` and `#channel` resolve to the same entry under ASCII case
102	mapping.
103	
104	---
105	
106	## Users
107	
108	### `IUser`
109	
110	Represents a user the bot is aware of (through shared channels or
111	direct interaction):
112	
113	```
114	IUser
115	├── Nick: string
116	├── User: string?            (ident / username)
117	├── Host: string?
118	├── Account: string?         (services account, from account-tag/extended-join/WHOX)
119	├── RealName: string?        (from extended-join or WHOIS)
120	├── IsAway: bool             (from away-notify)
121	├── AwayMessage: string?
122	├── IsBot: bool              (from bot tag or bot mode)
123	├── Channels: IReadOnlyCollection<IChannel>  (shared channels)
124	└── Hostmask: string         ("nick!user@host")
125	```
126	
127	### User Identity
128	
129	Users are identified primarily by nick (case-mapped per CASEMAPPING).
130	When `account-tag` is negotiated, the `Account` property provides a
131	stable identity across nick changes.
132	
133	### Concurrency Model
134	
135	`IUser` and `IChannel` objects are mutable — properties are updated
136	in place by the message processor when state changes occur (NICK,
137	CHGHOST, AWAY, MODE, etc.). Plugins holding a reference to an
138	`IUser` or `IChannel` see live updates.
139	
140	Thread safety guarantees:
141	
142	- **Individual property reads are atomic.** Reading a single property
143	  (e.g., `user.Nick`) always returns a consistent value — you will
144	  never see a partial write.
145	- **Cross-property consistency is not guaranteed.** If you read
146	  `user.Nick` and then `user.Host` in the same handler, a NICK or
147	  CHGHOST change could land between the two reads. In practice this
148	  is rare and usually harmless. Plugins that need strict consistency
149	  across multiple properties should copy the values they need into
150	  locals at the start of their handler.
151	- **Collection enumeration is safe.** Backing collections use
152	  `ConcurrentDictionary`, which safely handles concurrent reads and
153	  writes. Iterating `Channel.Members` while the message processor
154	  adds or removes a member will not throw.
155	
156	The user store uses `ConcurrentDictionary<string, IUser>` with
157	case-mapped keys. The channel store is similarly structured. On
158	disconnection, all state stores are cleared — plugins should treat
159	any cached references as stale after `OnDisconnectedAsync`.
160	
161	### The Bot's Own Identity
162	
163	The bot itself is represented as a special `IUser` accessible via
164	`IBot.Self`. This allows plugins to check the bot's current nick,
165	modes, and other properties.
166	
167	---
168	
169	## Capabilities
170	
171	### `ICapabilityManager`
172	
173	Plugins can query which IRCv3 capabilities the server supports and
174	which have been successfully negotiated:
175	
176	```
177	ICapabilityManager
178	├── IsNegotiated(string cap): bool       ("Is echo-message active?")
179	├── IsAvailable(string cap): bool        ("Does the server support it?")
180	├── NegotiatedCapabilities: IReadOnlySet<string>
181	├── AvailableCapabilities: IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string?>  (cap → value)
182	└── event CapabilitiesChanged            (from cap-notify)
183	```
184	
185	### Usage Pattern
186	
187	Plugins should check capability availability before relying on
188	cap-dependent features:
189	
190	```csharp
191	if (capabilities.IsNegotiated("echo-message"))
192	{
193	    // We will see our own messages echoed back — no need to
194	    // self-track what we send.
195	}
196	else
197	{
198	    // We need to manually track our outgoing messages.
199	}
200	```
201	
202	### Capability Constants
203	
204	`Marv.Core` provides string constants for all known capabilities to
205	avoid magic strings:
206	
207	```csharp
208	static class Capabilities
209	{
210	    public const string MessageTags = "message-tags";
211	    public const string ServerTime = "server-time";
212	    public const string EchoMessage = "echo-message";
213	    public const string AccountTag = "account-tag";
214	    public const string LabeledResponse = "labeled-response";
215	    // ... etc.
216	}
217	```
218	
219	---
220	
221	## ISUPPORT Parameters
222	
223	### `IServerInfo`
224	
225	The server advertises its configuration through ISUPPORT (005)
226	numerics. This information is available to plugins via `IServerInfo`:
227	
228	```
229	IServerInfo
230	├── NetworkName: string?
231	├── CaseMapping: CaseMapping          (Rfc1459, StrictRfc1459, Ascii)
232	├── ChannelModes: ChannelModeTypes    (A/B/C/D mode classifications)
233	├── Prefix: PrefixMapping             (mode char → prefix char, e.g. o→@)
234	├── MaxChannels: int?
235	├── MaxNickLength: int?
236	├── MaxTopicLength: int?
237	├── MaxMessageLength: int             (default 512 minus overhead)
238	├── ChannelTypes: IReadOnlySet<char>  ('#', '&', etc.)
239	├── Supports(string token): bool
240	└── GetValue(string token): string?
241	```
242	
243	This is updated live as the server sends ISUPPORT messages (including
244	resends after connection registration).
245	
246	---
247	
248	## Events
249	
250	### Event Hierarchy
251	
252	Events are strongly-typed classes organized by category. Each event
253	carries the relevant context and the raw `IrcMessage` for advanced use.
254	
255	#### Connection Events
256	
257	| Event | When |
258	|---|---|
259	| `ConnectedEvent` | IRC registration complete (001 received) |
260	| `DisconnectedEvent` | Connection lost or closed |
261	| `CapabilitiesChangedEvent` | Capabilities added/removed at runtime (cap-notify) |
262	
263	#### Message Events
264	
265	Message events use a unified type with an `IsDirect` flag rather than
266	separate channel/private variants. This halves the event type count
267	and means a handler that doesn't care about the distinction (which is
268	common) needs only one subscription.
269	
270	| Event | Key Properties |
271	|---|---|
272	| `MessageEvent` | `Channel?`, `Sender`, `Text`, `IsDirect`, `ReplyTo?` |
273	| `NoticeEvent` | `Channel?`, `Sender`, `Text`, `IsDirect` |
274	| `ActionEvent` | `Channel?`, `Sender`, `Text`, `IsDirect` |
275	| `CtcpEvent` | `Sender`, `Command`, `Args?`, `IsDirect` |
276	
277	When `IsDirect` is true, `Channel` is null (the message was sent
278	directly to the bot). When `IsDirect` is false, `Channel` identifies
279	the channel the message was sent to.
280	
281	**CTCP handling**: The core handles CTCP VERSION, PING, and TIME
282	automatically — responses are generated by the message processor
283	without plugin involvement. The VERSION response includes only the
284	bot's name and version string (no host or OS information). All other
285	CTCP queries (e.g., SOURCE, USERINFO, custom) are translated into
286	`CtcpEvent` for plugins to handle. `CtcpEvent.Command` is the CTCP
287	command name (e.g., `"SOURCE"`), and `CtcpEvent.Args` contains any
288	arguments after the command. ACTION is handled separately as
289	`ActionEvent` and is not dispatched as `CtcpEvent`.
290	
291	#### Channel Events
292	
293	| Event | Key Properties |
294	|---|---|
295	| `UserJoinedEvent` | `Channel`, `User`, `Account?` |
296	| `UserPartedEvent` | `Channel`, `User`, `Reason?` |
297	| `UserKickedEvent` | `Channel`, `Kicker`, `Kicked`, `Reason?` |
298	| `TopicChangedEvent` | `Channel`, `SetBy`, `NewTopic` |
299	| `ModeChangedEvent` | `Channel`, `SetBy`, `Changes` |
300	| `InviteReceivedEvent` | `Channel`, `InvitedBy` |
301	
302	#### User Events
303	
304	| Event | Key Properties |
305	|---|---|
306	| `UserQuitEvent` | `User`, `Reason?`, `AffectedChannels` |
307	| `NickChangedEvent` | `User`, `OldNick`, `NewNick` |
308	| `AccountChangedEvent` | `User`, `OldAccount?`, `NewAccount?` |
309	| `AwayChangedEvent` | `User`, `IsAway`, `Message?` |
310	| `HostChangedEvent` | `User`, `OldHost`, `NewHost` |
311	
312	#### Raw Protocol Event
313	
314	| Event | Key Properties |
315	|---|---|
316	| `RawMessageEvent` | `Message: IrcMessage` |
317	
318	The `RawMessageEvent` fires for every inbound message, before any
319	higher-level event is dispatched. Plugins that need to handle protocol
320	messages not covered by the typed events can subscribe to this.
321	
322	### Common Event Properties
323	
324	All events inherit from `MarvEvent` and share:
325	
326	```
327	MarvEvent
328	├── Timestamp: DateTimeOffset   (from server-time tag, or local clock)
329	├── RawMessage: IrcMessage      (the underlying protocol message)
330	├── MessageId: string?          (from msgid tag)
331	└── BatchId: string?            (from batch tag, null if not batched)
332	```
333	
334	### Batched Messages
335	
336	When the server sends a `BATCH` group, individual messages within the
337	batch are delivered as normal typed events — each carries a `BatchId`
338	property linking it to the batch. Plugins that don't care about
339	batching simply ignore `BatchId` and process events normally.
340	
341	Plugins that need batch-aware processing (e.g., collecting all
342	netsplit QUITs atomically) can subscribe to the batch start/end
343	signals and collect events by `BatchId`:
344	
345	| Event | Key Properties |
346	|---|---|
347	| `BatchStartEvent` | `BatchId`, `Type`, `Parameters` |
348	| `BatchEndEvent` | `BatchId` |
349	
350	This approach avoids forcing all plugin authors to handle both
351	individual and batched event delivery. The default path (ignore
352	batching) works without any special handling.
353	
354	### Event Ordering
355	
356	Events are dispatched per-plugin (each plugin has its own channel and
357	task — see `architecture.md`). The ordering guarantees are:
358	
359	1. State tracking updates happen before events are fanned out
360	2. `RawMessageEvent` is dispatched before the corresponding typed
361	   event
362	3. Within a single plugin, events arrive in the order the server
363	   sent them
364	4. Across plugins, events are delivered concurrently — there is no
365	   ordering guarantee between different plugins' handling of the same
366	   event
367
Tool reply2026-05-30T14:45:19.352Z
1	# Plugin API Draft
2	
3	This document describes the types, patterns, and APIs that plugin
4	authors work with when building Marv plugins.
5	
6	---
7	
8	## Core Types for Plugin Authors
9	
10	A plugin author's day-to-day involves these types from `Marv.Core`:
11	
12	| Type | Purpose |
13	|---|---|
14	| `IPlugin` | Interface defining the plugin contract |
15	| `MarvPlugin` | Convenience base class implementing `IPlugin` |
16	| `IBot` | Facade for sending messages and querying state |
17	| `IChannel`, `IUser` | Read-only state models |
18	| `ICapabilityManager` | Check negotiated capabilities |
19	| `IServerInfo` | Server configuration (ISUPPORT) |
20	| `[PluginConfig]` | Attribute for configuration classes |
21	| Event classes (`MessageEvent`, etc.) | Typed event payloads |
22	| Attributes (`[OnEvent]`, `[OnCommand]`, etc.) | Declare event interest |
23	| `[ProvidesService]` | Declare a service this plugin provides |
24	| `[DependsOn]` | Explicit plugin ordering |
25	
26	---
27	
28	## Plugin Lifecycle
29	
30	### IPlugin Interface
31	
32	The `IPlugin` interface defines the full plugin contract. All
33	plugins must implement it — either directly or via the `MarvPlugin`
34	convenience base class.
35	
36	```csharp
37	public interface IPlugin
38	{
39	    /// Human-readable name for this plugin. Used in log messages,
40	    /// configuration, and diagnostics. Enforced at compile time
41	    /// via static abstract.
42	    static abstract string PluginName { get; }
43	
44	    /// Called once after the plugin is constructed and all services
45	    /// are available. Use for one-time initialization.
46	    Task OnLoadAsync(CancellationToken ct);
47	
48	    /// Called each time the bot establishes an IRC connection.
49	    Task OnConnectedAsync(CancellationToken ct);
50	
51	    /// Called when the IRC connection is lost. The bot may reconnect.
52	    /// Any cached IChannel/IUser references are stale after this call.
53	    Task OnDisconnectedAsync();
54	
55	    /// Called once during shutdown, before the DI container is disposed.
56	    /// Use for cleanup (unsubscribe, flush, close handles).
57	    Task OnUnloadAsync();
58	
59	    /// Called by the core's per-plugin event loop to deliver an event.
60	    /// The core calls this method once per event, sequentially — never
61	    /// concurrently with itself for the same plugin.
62	    Task HandleEventAsync(MarvEvent evt, CancellationToken ct);
63	
64	    /// Called during DI container setup to register services this
65	    /// plugin provides. Only plugins that provide services to other
66	    /// plugins need to override this. Default implementation is a
67	    /// no-op.
68	    static virtual void ConfigureServices(IServiceCollection services) { }
69	}
70	```
71	
72	The core's per-plugin event loop reads from the plugin's
73	`Channel<MarvEvent>` and calls `HandleEventAsync` for each event.
74	This is the single entry point for all event delivery — the core
75	never calls handler methods directly.
76	
77	### IPluginActivator
78	
79	`MarvPlugin` needs to create handler group instances at runtime.
80	`IPluginActivator` wraps `IServiceProvider` and
81	`ActivatorUtilities.CreateInstance` behind a focused interface:
82	
83	```csharp
84	public interface IPluginActivator
85	{
86	    /// Creates an instance of T, injecting constructor parameters
87	    /// from the DI container. Additional parameters can be passed
88	    /// to satisfy constructor arguments not registered in DI.
89	    T CreateInstance<T>(params object[] parameters);
90	}
91	```
92	
93	This is intentionally limited to instance creation — it is not a
94	general service locator. The internal implementation delegates to
95	`ActivatorUtilities.CreateInstance<T>(IServiceProvider, params object[])`.
96	
97	### MarvPlugin Base Class
98	
99	Most plugins should extend `MarvPlugin`, which provides default
100	(no-op) lifecycle implementations, `IBot` access, and
101	reflection-based event dispatch to attributed handler methods:
102	
103	```csharp
104	public abstract class MarvPlugin : IPlugin
105	{
106	    /// The bot instance, available to all plugins.
107	    protected IBot Bot { get; }
108	
109	    /// Derived plugins accept IBot and IPluginActivator, and forward
110	    /// both via : base(bot, activator).
111	    protected MarvPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator)
112	    {
113	        Bot = bot;
114	        // Discovers [HandlerGroup] types for this plugin in the
115	        // assembly, creates instances via activator, and builds
116	        // the handler dispatch table from attributed methods on
117	        // both this plugin and its handler groups.
118	    }
119	
120	    /// Dispatches the event to matching handler methods discovered
121	    /// during construction. Handlers are called consecutively in
122	    /// an undefined order.
123	    public virtual Task HandleEventAsync(MarvEvent evt, CancellationToken ct)
124	    {
125	        // Reflection-based dispatch:
126	        // 1. Match evt type against [OnEvent] method parameters
127	        // 2. Match [OnCommand], [OnRegex] against MessageEvent text
128	        // 3. Match [OnRawMessage] against RawMessageEvent commands
129	        // 4. Call matching handlers on this plugin and handler groups
130	    }
131	
132	    /// Default lifecycle implementations forward to handler groups.
133	    /// Override these in derived classes, but call base to propagate
134	    /// lifecycle events to handler groups.
135	    public virtual Task OnLoadAsync(CancellationToken ct)
136	    {
137	        // Calls OnLoadAsync on each handler group (if defined)
138	    }
139	    public virtual Task OnConnectedAsync(CancellationToken ct) => /* forwards to groups */;
140	    public virtual Task OnDisconnectedAsync() => /* forwards to groups */;
141	    public virtual Task OnUnloadAsync() => /* forwards to groups */;
142	}
143	```
144	
145	Each plugin assembly must contain exactly one `IPlugin`
146	implementation. The `PluginName` static property identifies the
147	plugin in log messages, the plugin loading configuration, and
148	diagnostic output. For `MarvPlugin` subclasses, `IBot` and
149	`IPluginActivator` are passed to the base constructor via
150	`: base(bot, activator)`.
151	
152	Plugins that need full control can implement `IPlugin` directly,
153	bypassing `MarvPlugin`. In this case, the plugin manages its own
154	`IBot` access (typically via constructor injection), implements all
155	lifecycle methods, and writes its own `HandleEventAsync` dispatch
156	logic. Handler methods on direct `IPlugin` implementations must be
157	`public` (same rule as handler groups).
158	
159	Plugins that need configuration declare a separate configuration
160	class tagged with `[PluginConfig(Section = "Name")]`. The plugin
161	loader discovers these during assembly scanning and registers
162	`IOptions<TConfig>` bound to the `Plugins:{Section}` configuration
163	section — no `ConfigureServices` boilerplate needed. Plugins access
164	their configuration via constructor injection of `IOptions<TConfig>`.
165	
166	### Lifecycle Order
167	
168	1. Constructor (DI injects config and services)
169	2. `OnLoadAsync` (in dependency order)
170	3. `OnConnectedAsync` (in dependency order, after each connect)
171	4. Event handlers run during the connection
172	5. `OnDisconnectedAsync` (reverse dependency order)
173	6. Steps 3–5 repeat on reconnection
174	7. `OnUnloadAsync` (reverse dependency order, once at shutdown)
175	
176	---
177	
178	## Registering Interest in Events
179	
180	Plugins declare event handlers using attributes on methods. The
181	method must be an instance method on the plugin class (or a handler
182	group class — see below) and return `Task`.
183	
184	**Visibility rules:**
185	
186	- **`MarvPlugin` subclasses**: Handler methods do not need to be
187	  public — the dispatch is performed from within the `MarvPlugin`
188	  base class, so `protected` and `private` methods are accessible.
189	- **Direct `IPlugin` implementations** and **handler group classes**:
190	  Handler methods must be `public`, since the dispatch code cannot
191	  access non-public members of a separate class.
192	
193	If multiple handler methods on the same plugin (or its handler
194	groups) match the same event, they are all called consecutively but
195	in an undefined order.
196	
197	### `[OnEvent]` — Subscribe to any typed event
198	
199	```csharp
200	[OnEvent]
201	public Task HandleJoin(UserJoinedEvent e, CancellationToken ct)
202	{
203	    // Runs when any user joins any channel the bot is in.
204	    return Task.CompletedTask;
205	}
206	```
207	
208	The event type is inferred from the method parameter. Only one event
209	parameter is allowed.
210	
211	### `[OnCommand]` — Respond to a user command
212	
213	```csharp
214	[OnCommand("hello")]
215	public async Task HandleHello(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
216	{
217	    await ctx.ReplyAsync($"Hello, {ctx.Sender.Nick}!");
218	}
219	```
220	
221	`CommandContext` extends the message event with parsed command
222	arguments:
223	
224	```
225	CommandContext
226	├── Command: string                 ("hello")
227	├── Args: IReadOnlyList<string>     (remaining words)
228	├── ArgString: string               (remaining text, unparsed)
229	├── Channel: IChannel?              (null for private messages)
230	├── Sender: IUser
231	├── IsDirect: bool
232	├── ReplyAsync(string): Task        (responds in-context)
233	└── RawMessage: IrcMessage
234	```
235	
236	The command prefix (e.g., `!` or `.`) is configured per-bot, not
237	per-plugin.
238	
239	### `[OnRegex]` — Match messages against a regular expression
240	
241	```csharp
242	[OnRegex(@"https?://\S+")]
243	public async Task HandleUrl(RegexMatchContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
244	{
245	    var url = ctx.Match.Value;
246	    await ctx.ReplyAsync($"I see a URL: {url}");
247	}
248	```
249	
250	`RegexMatchContext` extends the message event with the regex match:
251	
252	```
253	RegexMatchContext
254	├── Match: Match                    (System.Text.RegularExpressions.Match)
255	├── Channel: IChannel?
256	├── Sender: IUser
257	├── IsDirect: bool
258	├── ReplyAsync(string): Task
259	└── RawMessage: IrcMessage
260	```
261	
262	The regex is matched against the full message text. If there are
263	multiple matches, the handler is called once with the first match.
264	Named capture groups are accessible via `ctx.Match.Groups["name"]`.
265	
266	### `[OnRawMessage]` — Subscribe to raw IRC commands
267	
268	```csharp
269	[OnRawMessage("INVITE")]
270	public async Task HandleInvite(IrcMessage message, CancellationToken ct)
271	{
272	    // Direct access to the raw protocol message.
273	}
274	```
275	
276	This is for protocol-level handling that the typed events don't cover.
277	
278	### `[OnInterval]` — Periodic tasks
279	
280	```csharp
281	[OnInterval(minutes: 5)]
282	public async Task CheckSomething(CancellationToken ct)
283	{
284	    // Runs every 5 minutes while connected.
285	}
286	```
287	
288	Interval handlers run on the plugin's own task.
289	
290	---
291	
292	## Handler Groups
293	
294	For plugins with many handlers, related handlers can be organized
295	into separate classes using `[HandlerGroup]`. A handler group is a
296	class whose methods are treated as if they were methods on the plugin
297	itself.
298	
299	```csharp
300	public class MyPlugin : MarvPlugin
301	{
302	    // Plugin-level lifecycle, minimal handler code here
303	}
304	
305	[HandlerGroup(typeof(MyPlugin))]
306	public class MyAdminHandlers
307	{
308	    private readonly IBot _bot;
309	    private readonly IAuthorizationService _auth;
310	
311	    // Constructor-injected via IPluginActivator, just like plugins
312	    public MyAdminHandlers(IBot bot, IAuthorizationService auth)
313	    {
314	        _bot = bot;
315	        _auth = auth;
316	    }
317	
318	    [OnCommand("kick")]
319	    public async Task HandleKick(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
320	    {
321	        if (!await _auth.IsAuthorizedAsync(ctx.Sender, "mod.kick", ct))
322	        {
323	            await ctx.ReplyAsync("Permission denied.");
324	            return;
325	        }
326	        // ... perform the kick
327	    }
328	
329	    [OnCommand("ban")]
330	    public async Task HandleBan(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
331	    {
332	        // ...
333	    }
334	}
335	```
336	
337	Handler groups are:
338	
339	- Discovered automatically by scanning the plugin's assembly for
340	  classes with `[HandlerGroup(typeof(MyPlugin))]`
341	- Created by `MarvPlugin` via `IPluginActivator` — they are not
342	  registered in the DI container. Constructor parameters are resolved
343	  from DI by `ActivatorUtilities`.
344	- Their event handlers are dispatched by `MarvPlugin`, not by the
345	  core — the core only calls `HandleEventAsync` on the plugin
346	- Run on the owning plugin's task, sequentially with the plugin's
347	  own handlers (order between handlers from different groups is
348	  undefined)
349	- May define lifecycle methods (`OnLoadAsync`, `OnConnectedAsync`,
350	  `OnDisconnectedAsync`, `OnUnloadAsync`) which are called by
351	  `MarvPlugin`'s lifecycle methods
352	- Useful for separating concerns without creating multiple plugins
353	  (which would each get their own task and independent event ordering)
354	
355	---
356	
357	## Registering a Service
358	
359	A plugin provides a service for other plugins by declaring
360	`[ProvidesService]` on its class and overriding the static
361	`ConfigureServices` method from `IPlugin`.
362	
363	### Example: Auth Service
364	
365	**Interface** (in the plugin assembly or a separate contracts assembly):
366	
367	```csharp
368	public interface IAuthorizationService
369	{
370	    Task<bool> IsAuthorizedAsync(IUser user, string permission, CancellationToken ct);
371	}
372	```
373	
374	**Plugin class**:
375	
376	```csharp
377	[ProvidesService(typeof(IAuthorizationService))]
378	public class AuthPlugin : MarvPlugin
379	{
380	    public static string PluginName => "Auth";
381	
382	    public AuthPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator)
383	        : base(bot, activator) { }
384	
385	    public static void ConfigureServices(IServiceCollection services)
386	    {
387	        services.AddSingleton<IAuthorizationService, AccountBasedAuthService>();
388	    }
389	}
390	```
391	
392	The `[ProvidesService]` attribute tells the dependency sorter that
393	`AuthPlugin` provides `IAuthorizationService`. Other plugins that
394	inject `IAuthorizationService` via their constructor are automatically
395	sorted to load after `AuthPlugin`.
396	
397	---
398	
399	## Declaring and Resolving Dependencies
400	
401	### Required Dependency
402	
403	Simply inject the service via the constructor:
404	
405	```csharp
406	public class ModerationPlugin : MarvPlugin
407	{
408	    private readonly IAuthorizationService _auth;
409	
410	    public ModerationPlugin(IBot bot, IAuthorizationService auth)
411	    {
412	        _auth = auth;
413	    }
414	
415	    [OnCommand("ban")]
416	    public async Task HandleBan(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
417	    {
418	        if (!await _auth.IsAuthorizedAsync(ctx.Sender, "moderation.ban", ct))
419	        {
420	            await ctx.ReplyAsync("You are not authorized to use this command.");
421	            return;
422	        }
423	        // ... perform the ban
424	    }
425	}
426	```
427	
428	If no plugin provides `IAuthorizationService`, `ModerationPlugin`
429	fails to load at startup with a clear error.
430	
431	### Optional Dependency
432	
433	Make the parameter nullable with a default of `null`. The dependency
434	sorter infers optionality from the nullability and default value —
435	no attribute needed:
436	
437	```csharp
438	public class GreetPlugin : MarvPlugin
439	{
440	    public static string PluginName => "Greet";
441	
442	    private readonly GreetPluginConfig _config;
443	    private readonly IAuthorizationService? _auth;
444	
445	    public GreetPlugin(
446	        IBot bot,
447	        IPluginActivator activator,
448	        IOptions<GreetPluginConfig> config,
449	        IAuthorizationService? auth = null) : base(bot, activator)
450	    {
451	        _config = config.Value;
452	        _auth = auth;
453	    }
454	
455	    [OnEvent]
456	    public async Task HandleJoin(UserJoinedEvent e, CancellationToken ct)
457	    {
458	        if (_auth is not null &&
459	            !await _auth.IsAuthorizedAsync(e.User, "greet.receive", ct))
460	        {
461	            return;
462	        }
463	
464	        var message = _config.GreetMessage.Replace("{nick}", e.User.Nick);
465	        await Bot.SendMessageAsync(e.Channel.Name, message, ct);
466	    }
467	}
468	```
469	
470	### Direct Plugin Dependency
471	
472	For cases where a plugin depends on another plugin directly (for load
473	ordering, not service consumption):
474	
475	```csharp
476	[DependsOn(typeof(AuthPlugin))]
477	public class AdminPlugin : MarvPlugin { ... }
478	```
479	
480	This ensures `AuthPlugin` loads before `AdminPlugin`.
481	
482	---
483	
484	## What the Bot Exposes to Plugins
485	
486	### `IBot` Interface
487	
488	```csharp
489	public interface IBot
490	{
491	    // --- Identity ---
492	    IUser Self { get; }
493	
494	    // --- Sending Messages ---
495	    Task SendMessageAsync(string target, string text, CancellationToken ct);
496	    Task SendNoticeAsync(string target, string text, CancellationToken ct);
497	    Task SendActionAsync(string target, string text, CancellationToken ct);
498	    Task SendRawAsync(IrcMessage message, CancellationToken ct);
499	
500	    // --- Channel Operations ---
501	    Task JoinAsync(string channel, string? key, CancellationToken ct);
502	    Task PartAsync(string channel, string? reason, CancellationToken ct);
503	
504	    // --- State ---
505	    IReadOnlyDictionary<string, IChannel> Channels { get; }
506	    IReadOnlyDictionary<string, IUser> Users { get; }
507	
508	    // --- Server Info ---
509	    IServerInfo ServerInfo { get; }
510	    ICapabilityManager Capabilities { get; }
511	
512	    // --- Advanced ---
513	    Task<IReadOnlyList<IrcMessage>> SendAndAwaitAsync(
514	        IrcMessage message, CancellationToken ct);
515	}
516	```
517	
518	**`Channels`**: Dictionary keyed by case-mapped channel name. Use the
519	indexer for O(1) lookup by name (e.g., `bot.Channels["#general"]`).
520	
521	**`Users`**: Dictionary keyed by case-mapped nick. Contains all users
522	the bot is aware of (through shared channels or direct interaction).
523	
524	**`SendAndAwaitAsync`**: Sends an IRC command and waits for the
525	server's correlated response. Uses the `labeled-response` IRCv3
526	capability to tag the outbound message with a unique label, then
527	collects all inbound messages bearing that label until the server
528	signals completion. Useful for commands like WHO, WHOIS, and LIST
529	where the response spans multiple messages. Falls back to
530	timeout-based correlation if `labeled-response` is not negotiated.
531	
532	**Thread safety**: All `Send*Async` methods, `JoinAsync`, `PartAsync`,
533	and `SendAndAwaitAsync` are thread-safe — they can be called from any
534	context (plugin tasks, background tasks, timers). The `Channels` and
535	`Users` dictionaries are read-safe from any plugin task (they use
536	concurrent-read-safe data structures updated only by the message
537	processor).
538	
539	### Convenience Methods on Event Objects
540	
541	Event objects provide contextual shortcuts:
542	
543	```csharp
544	// On MessageEvent:
545	await e.ReplyAsync("response");    // sends to channel or DM, matching context
546	
547	// On UserJoinedEvent:
548	await Bot.SendMessageAsync(e.Channel.Name, "Welcome!", ct);
549	```
550	
551	`ReplyAsync` delegates to `IBot` internally, choosing the correct
552	target (channel or sender nick) based on context.
553	
554	---
555	
556	## Complete Examples
557	
558	### Simplest Possible Plugin
559	
560	A plugin that responds to `!ping` with `pong`:
561	
562	```csharp
563	public class PingPlugin : MarvPlugin
564	{
565	    public static string PluginName => "Ping";
566	
567	    public PingPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator)
568	        : base(bot, activator) { }
569	
570	    [OnCommand("ping")]
571	    public async Task HandlePing(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
572	    {
573	        await ctx.ReplyAsync("pong");
574	    }
575	}
576	```
577	
578	No configuration, no services, no lifecycle hooks, no
579	`ConfigureServices`. Discovered by assembly scanning and wired up
580	automatically.
581	
582	### Plugin with Configuration
583	
584	A greeting plugin with configurable messages:
585	
586	```csharp
587	[PluginConfig(Section = "Greet")]
588	public record GreetPluginConfig
589	{
590	    public string GreetMessage { get; init; } = "Welcome, {nick}!";
591	    public bool GreetOnJoin { get; init; } = true;
592	}
593	
594	public class GreetPlugin : MarvPlugin
595	{
596	    public static string PluginName => "Greet";
597	
598	    private readonly GreetPluginConfig _config;
599	
600	    public GreetPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator,
601	        IOptions<GreetPluginConfig> config) : base(bot, activator)
602	    {
603	        _config = config.Value;
604	    }
605	
606	    [OnEvent]
607	    public async Task HandleJoin(UserJoinedEvent e, CancellationToken ct)
608	    {
609	        if (!_config.GreetOnJoin)
610	            return;
611	
612	        var message = _config.GreetMessage.Replace("{nick}", e.User.Nick);
613	        await Bot.SendMessageAsync(e.Channel.Name, message, ct);
614	    }
615	}
616	```
617	
618	Configuration is automatically bound to `Plugins:Greet` in the
619	config file (from the `[PluginConfig]` attribute's `Section`). No
620	`ConfigureServices` needed.
621	
622	### Plugin That Provides and Consumes a Service
623	
624	An auth plugin providing `IAuthorizationService`, consumed by a
625	moderation plugin:
626	
627	```csharp
628	// --- Contracts (could be in a separate assembly) ---
629	
630	public interface IAuthorizationService
631	{
632	    Task<bool> IsAuthorizedAsync(IUser user, string permission, CancellationToken ct);
633	}
634	
635	// --- Auth Plugin ---
636	
637	[PluginConfig(Section = "Auth")]
638	public record AuthPluginConfig
639	{
640	    public List<string> AdminAccounts { get; init; } = [];
641	}
642	
643	[ProvidesService(typeof(IAuthorizationService))]
644	public class AuthPlugin : MarvPlugin
645	{
646	    public static string PluginName => "Auth";
647	
648	    public AuthPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator)
649	        : base(bot, activator) { }
650	
651	    public static void ConfigureServices(IServiceCollection services)
652	    {
653	        services.AddSingleton<IAuthorizationService, AccountBasedAuthService>();
654	    }
655	}
656	
657	internal class AccountBasedAuthService : IAuthorizationService
658	{
659	    private readonly IOptions<AuthPluginConfig> _config;
660	
661	    public AccountBasedAuthService(IOptions<AuthPluginConfig> config)
662	    {
663	        _config = config;
664	    }
665	
666	    public Task<bool> IsAuthorizedAsync(
667	        IUser user, string permission, CancellationToken ct)
668	    {
669	        var isAdmin = user.Account is not null &&
670	            _config.Value.AdminAccounts.Contains(user.Account);
671	        return Task.FromResult(isAdmin);
672	    }
673	}
674	
675	// --- Moderation Plugin (consumes auth, required) ---
676	
677	public class ModerationPlugin : MarvPlugin
678	{
679	    public static string PluginName => "Moderation";
680	
681	    private readonly IAuthorizationService _auth;
682	
683	    public ModerationPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator,
684	        IAuthorizationService auth) : base(bot, activator)
685	    {
686	        _auth = auth;
687	    }
688	
689	    [OnCommand("kick")]
690	    public async Task HandleKick(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct)
691	    {
692	        if (!await _auth.IsAuthorizedAsync(ctx.Sender, "mod.kick", ct))
693	        {
694	            await ctx.ReplyAsync("Permission denied.");
695	            return;
696	        }
697	
698	        if (ctx.Args.Count == 0)
699	        {
700	            await ctx.ReplyAsync("Usage: !kick <nick> [reason]");
701	            return;
702	        }
703	
704	        var targetNick = ctx.Args[0];
705	        var reason = ctx.Args.Count > 1
706	            ? string.Join(' ', ctx.Args.Skip(1))
707	            : "Kicked by moderator";
708	
709	        await Bot.SendRawAsync(
710	            new IrcMessage("KICK", [ctx.Channel!.Name, targetNick, reason]),
711	            ct);
712	    }
713	}
714	```
715	
716	### Plugin with Handler Groups
717	
718	A moderation plugin that organizes handlers by concern:
719	
720	```csharp
721	public class ModerationPlugin : MarvPlugin
722	{
723	    public static string PluginName => "Moderation";
724	
725	    private readonly IAuthorizationService _auth;
726	
727	    public ModerationPlugin(IBot bot, IPluginActivator activator,
728	        IAuthorizationService auth) : base(bot, activator)
729	    {
730	        _auth = auth;
731	    }
732	}
733	
734	[HandlerGroup(typeof(ModerationPlugin))]
735	public class KickBanHandlers
736	{
737	    private readonly IBot _bot;
738	    private readonly IAuthorizationService _auth;
739	
740	    public KickBanHandlers(IBot bot, IAuthorizationService auth)
741	    {
742	        _bot = bot;
743	        _auth = auth;
744	    }
745	
746	    [OnCommand("kick")]
747	    public async Task HandleKick(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct) { ... }
748	
749	    [OnCommand("ban")]
750	    public async Task HandleBan(CommandContext ctx, CancellationToken ct) { ... }
751	
752	    // Handler groups can also have lifecycle methods
753	    public Task OnConnectedAsync(CancellationToken ct) => Task.CompletedTask;
754	}
755	
756	[HandlerGroup(typeof(ModerationPlugin))]
757	public class FloodProtectionHandlers
758	{
759	    [OnEvent]
760	    public async Task HandleMessage(MessageEvent e, CancellationToken ct) { ... }
761	}
762	```
763	
764	---
765	
766	## Plugin Project Structure
767	
768	A typical plugin project on disk:
769	
770	```
771	Marv.Plugins.MyPlugin/
772	├── Marv.Plugins.MyPlugin.csproj   (references Marv.Core)
773	├── MyPlugin.cs                    (plugin class)
774	├── MyPluginConfig.cs              (configuration record, if needed)
775	├── Services/
776	│   ├── IMyService.cs              (service interface, if providing)
777	│   └── MyService.cs               (service implementation)
778	└── Handlers/
779	    ├── AdminHandlers.cs           (handler group)
780	    └── UserHandlers.cs            (handler group)
781	```
782	
783	The `.csproj` references `Marv.Core` and nothing else from the Marv
784	solution (unless consuming another plugin's contracts assembly).
785	
786	---
787	
788	## Testing Plugins
789	
790	Plugins are testable without a running bot:
791	
792	```csharp
793	[Fact]
794	public async Task PingPlugin_responds_with_pong()
795	{
796	    var plugin = new PingPlugin();
797	    var ctx = CommandContextFake.Create(command: "ping", sender: "testuser");
798	
799	    await plugin.HandlePing(ctx, CancellationToken.None);
800	
801	    Assert.Equal("pong", ctx.Replies.Single());
802	}
803	```
804	
805	`Marv.Core` provides test fakes (`CommandContextFake`, `ChannelFake`,
806	`UserFake`, `BotFake`) so plugin authors can unit test handlers
807	without mocking infrastructure.

Tool reply2026-05-30T14:45:39.282Z
1	# ADR-001: Platform Abstraction
2	
3	**Status**: Proposed  
4	**Date**: 2026-05-30
5	
6	## Context
7	
8	Marv targets IRC exclusively and does not need to support other chat
9	platforms. However, the raw IRC protocol is a poor API surface for
10	plugin authors — it requires knowledge of protocol details, manual
11	string parsing, and careful state tracking.
12	
13	We need to decide how much abstraction to place between the IRC wire
14	protocol and the plugin API.
15	
16	The spectrum of options:
17	
18	1. **No abstraction**: Plugins work with raw `IrcMessage` objects and
19	   must parse parameters, track state, and handle protocol quirks
20	   themselves.
21	
22	2. **Thin IRC-specific models**: Typed models for IRC concepts
23	   (channels, users, messages) that directly reflect IRC semantics
24	   but hide parsing details.
25	
26	3. **Platform-agnostic abstraction**: Generic chat concepts
27	   (conversations, participants, messages) designed for
28	   multi-platform portability, as frameworks like Errbot do.
29	
30	## Decision
31	
32	**Option 2: Thin IRC-specific models.**
33	
34	We model IRC's entities — channels, users, messages, modes,
35	capabilities — as first-class types with typed properties and
36	consistent APIs, but we do not abstract away IRC's semantics. A
37	channel is an `IChannel` (not a "conversation"), a user is an `IUser`
38	(not a "participant"), and capabilities are IRCv3 capabilities (not
39	generic "platform features").
40	
41	Plugins work with these typed models in event handlers and queries.
42	Raw `IrcMessage` access is always available via the `RawMessage`
43	property on events, and plugins can subscribe to `RawMessageEvent` for
44	protocol-level handling.
45	
46	## Rationale
47	
48	**Why not option 1 (no abstraction)**: Forcing every plugin to parse
49	PRIVMSG parameters, track nick changes, handle case mapping, and
50	manage channel membership would violate the DX design goal. The same
51	state-tracking code would be duplicated across every non-trivial
52	plugin.
53	
54	**Why not option 3 (platform-agnostic)**: Marv will only support IRC.
55	Building a platform-agnostic abstraction layer adds complexity without
56	delivering value:
57	
58	- IRC-specific features (modes, prefixes, CTCP, capabilities) would
59	  need to leak through the abstraction anyway, creating a worst-of-
60	  both-worlds API.
61	- Plugin authors targeting Marv are writing for IRC. An abstraction
62	  that hides IRC semantics makes their job harder, not easier.
63	- If multi-platform support is ever desired, it would be better to
64	  build it as a separate project that composes protocol-specific bots,
65	  rather than burdening the IRC bot with abstractions it doesn't need.
66	
67	**Why option 2 works**: IRC's concepts are well-defined and stable.
68	Channels, users, modes, and messages have clear semantics that map
69	directly to typed models. The abstraction removes boilerplate (parsing,
70	state tracking, case mapping) while preserving the mental model that
71	IRC-knowledgeable plugin authors already have.
72	
73	## Consequences
74	
75	- Plugin authors must understand IRC concepts (channels, nicks, modes)
76	  to be productive. This is acceptable because anyone writing an IRC
77	  bot plugin is expected to understand IRC.
78	- If Marv ever needs to support a non-IRC platform, the plugin API
79	  would need significant changes. We accept this tradeoff — YAGNI.
80	- The raw `IrcMessage` escape hatch ensures that no IRC functionality
81	  is inaccessible, even if the typed models don't cover every edge
82	  case.
83	- The typed models serve as a natural place to centralize correct
84	  handling of protocol quirks (case mapping, mode parsing, message
85	  length limits).
86
