1	# ADR-002: IRC Library Choice
2	
3	**Status**: Proposed  
4	**Date**: 2026-05-30
5	
6	## Context
7	
8	Marv needs to parse IRC messages, manage a TCP/TLS connection,
9	negotiate IRCv3 capabilities, and track channel/user state. We
10	evaluated whether to use an existing C# IRC library or build our own
11	protocol layer.
12	
13	Five libraries were evaluated (see `docs/research.md` section 1):
14	
15	| Library | Target | IRCv3 | Maintained | Verdict |
16	|---|---|---|---|---|
17	| ChatSharp | .NET Framework | Partial | No | Unsuitable |
18	| IRC.NET (IrcDotNet) | .NET Framework 4.0 | None | No | Unsuitable |
19	| NetIRC | .NET Standard 2.0 | None | Low | Unsuitable |
20	| IrcNet (NowaLone) | .NET 6+ | Unclear | Too new | Unsuitable |
21	
22	## Decision
23	
24	**Build our own IRC protocol layer**, consisting of:
25	
26	1. An `IrcParser` for parsing raw messages into `IrcMessage` records,
27	   validated against the ircdocs/parser-tests test vectors.
28	2. An `IrcSerializer` for converting `IrcMessage` back to wire format.
29	3. A `CaseMapping` utility implementing RFC 1459, strict-RFC 1459, and
30	   ASCII case folding.
31	4. An `IIrcConnection` managing TCP/TLS, reconnection, rate limiting,
32	   and PING/PONG.
33	5. A `CapabilityEngine` for IRCv3 CAP negotiation and SASL
34	   authentication.
35	6. State tracking for channels, users, and modes.
36	
37	We do not take a dependency on any existing IRC library.
38	
39	## Rationale
40	
41	**No suitable library exists.** The C# IRC library ecosystem is
42	fragmented between abandoned projects targeting legacy .NET and
43	immature projects with unclear IRCv3 coverage. None of the candidates
44	meet Marv's requirements:
45	
46	- Target .NET 10 (or at least .NET Standard 2.1+)
47	- Comprehensive IRCv3 capability support (message-tags, labeled-
48	  response, batch, echo-message, etc.)
49	- Async/await-first API
50	- Active maintenance
51	
52	**The IRC protocol is tractable to implement.** The wire protocol is
53	text-based with well-documented parsing rules. Community-maintained
54	test vectors (ircdocs/parser-tests) provide comprehensive coverage for
55	the parser. The hard parts — capability negotiation, state tracking,
56	mode parsing — are not handled well by any existing library anyway.
57	
58	**Full control over the async model.** Building our own connection
59	layer lets us use `System.Threading.Channels` for internal message
60	passing and control the threading model precisely (see ADR-004).
61	Wrapping an existing library's event model would add friction and
62	limit our design options.
63	
64	**No maintenance risk from upstream.** Given that every candidate
65	library is either abandoned or has zero community adoption, depending
66	on one would add risk rather than reduce it.
67	
68	## Consequences
69	
70	- We own the parser, connection management, and capability negotiation
71	  code. This is more initial work but eliminates external dependency
72	  risk.
73	- We must validate the parser against ircdocs/parser-tests and
74	  maintain correctness as the IRC protocol evolves.
75	- The common failure modes documented in `docs/research.md` section 5
76	  must be addressed in our implementation and test suite.
77	- If a high-quality C# IRC library emerges in the future, the
78	  protocol layer is internal to `Marv.Core` and could be replaced
79	  without affecting the plugin API.
80

1	# ADR-003: Plugin Service Registry
2	
3	**Status**: Proposed  
4	**Date**: 2026-05-30
5	
6	## Context
7	
8	Marv's plugin system needs to support inter-plugin services: one
9	plugin provides an implementation (e.g., `IAuthorizationService`),
10	and other plugins consume it. We need to decide how services are
11	registered, discovered, and resolved.
12	
13	Three approaches were evaluated (see `docs/research.md` section 4):
14	
15	1. **.NET `IServiceProvider`** (Microsoft DI) — plugins register into
16	   `IServiceCollection`, the container is built once, dependencies are
17	   constructor-injected.
18	
19	2. **Explicit service registry** — a custom `IServiceRegistry` where
20	   plugins call `Register<T>()` and `Get<T>()` at runtime.
21	
22	3. **Hybrid** — Microsoft DI as the underlying mechanism, wrapped in
23	   a plugin-aware metadata layer for dependency sorting and
24	   diagnostics.
25	
26	## Decision
27	
28	**Option 3: Hybrid approach with a single DI container.**
29	
30	There is one `IServiceCollection` and one `IServiceProvider` for the
31	entire application. Core services and plugin-contributed services all
32	live in the same container. There is no separate container for
33	plugins.
34	
35	### Service Discovery
36	
37	**Provided services** are declared explicitly with a
38	`[ProvidesService(typeof(IFoo))]` attribute on the plugin class. The
39	plugin overrides the static `ConfigureServices(IServiceCollection)`
40	method defined on `IPlugin` (which has an empty default
41	implementation) to perform the actual DI registration. The attribute
42	tells the dependency sorter which plugin provides which service; the
43	method does the wiring.
44	
45	**Consumed services** are discovered automatically by inspecting the
46	plugin's constructor parameters. Any parameter whose type is not a
47	core service (not `IBot`, `IOptions<T>`, `ILogger<T>`, etc.) is
48	assumed to be a plugin-provided service dependency:
49	
50	- **Required**: Non-nullable parameter → the service must be
51	  registered by another plugin, or startup fails.
52	- **Optional**: Nullable parameter with a default of `null` → the
53	  service is used if available but does not block startup.
54	
55	Only the providing side needs an attribute. The consuming side is
56	inferred entirely from constructor signatures — nullability and
57	default values express optionality naturally. This keeps the common
58	case (consuming a service) completely boilerplate-free, while keeping
59	the uncommon case (providing a service) explicit and unambiguous.
60	
61	### Explicit Ordering
62	
63	`[DependsOn(typeof(OtherPlugin))]` forces one plugin to load after
64	another without implying any service relationship. This is a
65	secondary mechanism for cases where the service graph alone doesn't
66	capture the needed ordering.
67	
68	### Automatic Configuration
69	
70	Configuration classes tagged with `[PluginConfig(Section = "Name")]`
71	are discovered during assembly scanning and automatically registered
72	as `IOptions<TConfig>` bound to `Plugins:{Section}`. No
73	`ConfigureServices` boilerplate is needed for the common case of a
74	plugin that only has configuration and event handlers. Plugins access
75	their configuration via constructor injection of `IOptions<TConfig>`.
76	
77	## Rationale
78	
79	**Why not pure Microsoft DI (option 1)**: Microsoft DI alone does not
80	understand plugin identity or dependency ordering. It cannot answer
81	"which plugin provides this service?" or "in what order should plugins
82	load?" We need the metadata layer for:
83	
84	- Correct load ordering (provider must register before consumer)
85	- Clear startup error messages ("ModerationPlugin requires
86	  IAuthorizationService, but no loaded plugin provides it")
87	- Diagnostics and introspection
88	
89	**Why not a custom registry (option 2)**: A custom service locator
90	would duplicate functionality that Microsoft DI already provides
91	(lifetime management, constructor injection, scoping) and would be
92	unfamiliar to .NET developers. It also hides dependencies — consumers
93	call `registry.Get<T>()` at arbitrary points rather than declaring
94	them in the constructor.
95	
96	**Why `[ProvidesService]` on the provider only**: The providing side
97	is the uncommon case — most plugins only consume services or handle
98	events. An explicit attribute here is low-cost and avoids the
99	alternatives (scanning `ConfigureServices` registrations via a
100	throwaway `ServiceCollection`, or scanning assemblies for interface
101	implementations — both fragile). The consuming side needs no attribute
102	because the dependency information already lives in the constructor
103	signature.
104	
105	Optionality is inferred from nullability and default values: a
106	nullable parameter with `= null` is optional, everything else is
107	required. No Marv-specific attribute is needed on the consuming side.
108	If a plugin ever needs "nullable but required," `[DependsOn]` covers
109	that edge case.
110	
111	**Why a single container**: One `IServiceProvider` for core and
112	plugins avoids type identity issues and complex cross-container
113	resolution. A plugin's `IAuthorizationService` is the same type
114	whether resolved by core code or another plugin.
115	
116	## Consequences
117	
118	- Plugin authors use familiar .NET DI patterns for most things.
119	- Most plugins (consumers) need zero Marv-specific attributes — just
120	  constructor parameters with standard C# nullability.
121	- Plugins providing services need `[ProvidesService]` and a
122	  `ConfigureServices` method — explicit but only for the uncommon
123	  case.
124	- The `ConfigureServices` method is static and runs before plugin
125	  instances exist, which means service registration cannot depend on
126	  runtime state. This is intentional — services should be
127	  deterministic based on configuration.
128	- The single-container approach means plugin assemblies must not have
129	  conflicting type definitions. This is enforced by using a shared
130	  `AssemblyLoadContext`.
131

Tool reply2026-05-30T14:45:40.365Z
1	# ADR-004: Async / Threading Model
2	
3	**Status**: Proposed  
4	**Date**: 2026-05-30
5	
6	## Context
7	
8	An IRC bot has several concurrent concerns:
9	
10	- Reading from the network (blocking I/O)
11	- Writing to the network (with rate limiting)
12	- Processing inbound messages (state updates, event dispatch)
13	- Running plugin handlers (which may do I/O of their own)
14	- Periodic tasks (timers, health checks)
15	
16	We need to decide how these concerns are distributed across threads
17	and tasks, and what concurrency guarantees plugins receive.
18	
19	## Decision
20	
21	**Per-plugin tasks with a central message processor, connected by
22	`System.Threading.Channels`.**
23	
24	### Architecture
25	
26	The system uses N+4 long-lived async tasks (where N is the number of
27	loaded plugins):
28	
29	1. **Read loop**: Reads raw lines from the TCP stream, parses them
30	   into `IrcMessage`, and writes them to an inbound
31	   `Channel<IrcMessage>`. This task does no processing beyond parsing.
32	
33	2. **Message processor**: Reads from the inbound channel. For each
34	   message:
35	   - Handles protocol-level concerns (PING/PONG, CAP negotiation)
36	   - Updates state tracking (channels, users, modes)
37	   - Translates raw messages into typed events
38	   - Fans out each event to every plugin's individual event channel
39	
40	3. **Plugin tasks** (one per plugin): Each plugin has its own
41	   `Channel<MarvEvent>` and a dedicated async task. The task reads
42	   events from the channel and calls `plugin.HandleEventAsync(event,
43	   ct)` for each one. The core never calls handler methods directly —
44	   dispatch is the plugin's responsibility (provided by `MarvPlugin`
45	   via reflection, or custom logic for direct `IPlugin` implementations).
46	
47	4. **Rate limiter**: Accepts outbound messages from any task and
48	   releases them to the outbound channel at a rate that respects the
49	   server's flood limits (token bucket algorithm).
50	
51	5. **Write loop**: Reads from the outbound channel and writes
52	   serialized messages to the TCP stream.
53	
54	### Concurrency Guarantees
55	
56	- **Within a plugin, `HandleEventAsync` is called sequentially.** The
57	  core never calls it concurrently with itself for the same plugin.
58	  This means a plugin author does not need to think about thread
59	  safety for the plugin's own state.
60	
61	- **Different plugins run concurrently.** Plugin A's handler for
62	  an event may be running at the same time as Plugin B's handler for
63	  the same (or a different) event.
64	
65	- **State stores are read-safe from any plugin task.** The message
66	  processor updates state before fanning out events. State models are
67	  mutable, with individual property reads being atomic and collections
68	  using `ConcurrentDictionary` for safe concurrent access. Since only
69	  the message processor writes, there is no write contention. Plugins
70	  can safely read `IBot.Channels` and `IBot.Users` from their event
71	  handlers. Cross-property consistency is not guaranteed — see the
72	  rationale section below.
73	
74	- **`IBot.SendAsync` (and its variants) is thread-safe.** It writes
75	  to the rate limiter's input channel, which supports concurrent
76	  writers.
77	
78	- **`CancellationToken` propagation**: All async methods accept a
79	  `CancellationToken`. The bot's top-level token is cancelled on
80	  shutdown, which drains the channels and allows tasks to exit cleanly.
81	
82	### Plugin Background Work
83	
84	A plugin that needs to do slow work (HTTP requests, database queries)
85	can:
86	
87	1. Start the work with `Task.Run` or an async call
88	2. When the work completes, use `IBot.SendAsync` to send any resulting
89	   messages
90	3. Safely read `IBot.Channels` and `IBot.Users` (these are read-safe)
91	
92	If a plugin needs to update its own internal state from background
93	work, it should use its own synchronization (e.g., a `Channel<T>`
94	that the event handler drains, or `lock`/`ConcurrentDictionary` for
95	simple cases).
96	
97	## Rationale
98	
99	### Why per-plugin tasks instead of a single dispatcher
100	
101	**Plugin isolation.** With a single message processor calling all
102	plugin handlers sequentially, a slow plugin blocks every other plugin
103	and stalls state tracking. Per-plugin tasks isolate plugins from each
104	other — a slow handler in Plugin A only delays Plugin A's subsequent
105	events.
106	
107	**Natural concurrency.** Plugins are independent units of
108	functionality. Running them concurrently matches the mental model:
109	the greeting plugin and the moderation plugin shouldn't need to wait
110	for each other.
111	
112	**Sequential within a plugin.** Within a single plugin,
113	`HandleEventAsync` is called sequentially — never concurrently with
114	itself. This preserves the key DX benefit: a plugin author doesn't
115	need to think about thread safety for their own state. The
116	concurrency boundary is between plugins, not within them.
117	
118	### Why state stores are read-safe
119	
120	The message processor updates state (channels, users, modes) before
121	fanning out events to plugin channels. Since writes happen on one
122	task and reads happen on plugin tasks, the data structures must
123	support concurrent reads. State models (`IUser`, `IChannel`) are
124	mutable objects with individual property reads being atomic
125	(reference type fields in .NET). Collection-valued properties use
126	`ConcurrentDictionary`, which supports safe concurrent enumeration.
127	
128	Cross-property consistency is not guaranteed — a state change could
129	land between two reads within a handler. This is acceptable because
130	the window is small and the practical impact is minimal. Plugins
131	needing strict consistency across multiple properties can copy values
132	into locals at handler entry.
133	
134	### Why System.Threading.Channels
135	
136	- **Bounded memory**: Channels can be bounded, providing natural
137	  backpressure if a plugin falls behind.
138	- **Async-native**: `ReadAsync` / `WriteAsync` integrate with
139	  `async/await` and `CancellationToken`.
140	- **High performance**: Minimal allocation, lock-free for
141	  single-producer/single-consumer channels.
142	- **Standard .NET**: No third-party dependency, familiar API.
143	- **Fan-out friendly**: The message processor writes to N plugin
144	  channels — `Channel<T>` handles this efficiently.
145	
146	### Why not Rx (System.Reactive)
147	
148	Rx provides powerful composition operators (merge, buffer, throttle)
149	but adds a dependency and a learning curve. The message flow in Marv
150	is simple: one inbound stream, state update, fan-out to N plugin
151	channels. Channels are sufficient and more predictable.
152	
153	### Why not a thread pool / Task.Run per event
154	
155	Dispatching each event to the thread pool would mean a plugin's
156	handlers could run concurrently with each other, requiring every
157	plugin to be thread-safe. Per-plugin channels preserve sequential
158	ordering within a plugin without sacrificing inter-plugin concurrency.
159	
160	## Consequences
161	
162	- A misbehaving plugin handler that blocks its task will only affect
163	  that plugin, not others. However, it will miss subsequent events
164	  (they queue up in its channel). Mitigation: log warnings if a
165	  handler takes longer than a configurable threshold; bounded channels
166	  with drop-oldest policy as a safety valve.
167	
168	- Plugins must not assume ordering relative to other plugins. If
169	  Plugin A and Plugin B both handle `UserJoinedEvent`, either one
170	  might process it first.
171	
172	- State models are mutable with atomic individual property reads.
173	  Cross-property consistency is not guaranteed during a handler, but
174	  this is rare in practice. This is an implementation concern inside
175	  `Marv.Core`, not a plugin concern — plugins just read properties
176	  normally.
177	
178	- The rate limiter prevents any plugin (or combination of plugins)
179	  from flooding the server, even if multiple plugins send messages
180	  concurrently.
181	
182	- Reconnection is handled by tearing down all tasks and
183	  reinstantiating all plugins. On disconnection, plugins receive
184	  `OnDisconnectedAsync` then `OnUnloadAsync`, and all state is
185	  discarded: pending `SendAndAwaitAsync` calls are cancelled (their
186	  `TaskCompletionSource` is faulted with a disconnection exception),
187	  outbound message queues are cleared, and channel/user state stores
188	  are reset. Plugin and handler group instances are discarded and
189	  fresh instances are created via `ActivatorUtilities`. This
190	  eliminates any possibility of stale references surviving a
191	  reconnection.
192
Tool reply2026-05-30T14:45:40.702Z
1	# Research Summary
2	
3	Research conducted 2026-05-30 to inform the design of Marv, a C# IRC bot
4	targeting .NET 10. This document covers IRC libraries, IRCv3 capabilities,
5	existing bot architectures, plugin service patterns, and common failure modes.
6	
7	---
8	
9	## 1. C# IRC Libraries on NuGet
10	
11	### Candidates Evaluated
12	
13	#### ChatSharp
14	
15	- **Repository**: <https://git.sr.ht/~sircmpwn/ChatSharp>
16	- **NuGet**: `ChatSharp` (v1.0.2)
17	- **License**: MIT
18	- **Target**: .NET Framework (not .NET Standard or modern .NET)
19	- **IRCv3 support**: Partial. Supports CAP negotiation, `msgid`, bot mode,
20	  `no-implicit-names`, SASL (v3.2), and STS. Does **not** support
21	  `message-tags`, `account-tag`, `labeled-response`, `echo-message`,
22	  `batch`, `server-time`, `multi-prefix`, `extended-join`, `away-notify`,
23	  `account-notify`, `cap-notify`, `invite-notify`, `setname`,
24	  `standard-replies`, `userhost-in-names`, or WHOX.
25	- **Maintenance**: Last commit appears to be several years old. The project
26	  is listed on the official IRCv3 libraries page but has not kept pace with
27	  the spec.
28	- **API quality**: Event-driven, no dependencies. Straightforward for basic
29	  bots but lacks async/await patterns and modern .NET idioms.
30	- **Verdict**: Unsuitable. Missing too many IRCv3 features, targets legacy
31	  .NET Framework, and appears unmaintained.
32	
33	#### IRC.NET (IrcDotNet)
34	
35	- **Repository**: <https://github.com/IrcDotNet/IrcDotNet>
36	- **NuGet**: `IrcDotNet`
37	- **License**: MIT
38	- **Target**: .NET Framework 4.0 / Silverlight 4.0 (with Mono support)
39	- **IRCv3 support**: None. Implements RFC 1459 and RFC 2812 only.
40	- **Maintenance**: Effectively abandoned. Multiple forks exist, none with
41	  significant activity. The original was designed for .NET Framework 4.0
42	  and Silverlight.
43	- **API quality**: Comprehensive RFC implementation, but the API is dated
44	  (event-based, no async/await, heavy use of `EventArgs` patterns).
45	- **Verdict**: Unsuitable. No IRCv3 support, unmaintained, legacy target
46	  framework.
47	
48	#### NetIRC
49	
50	- **Repository**: <https://github.com/fredimachado/NetIRC>
51	- **NuGet**: `NetIRC` (v1.1.2)
52	- **License**: MIT
53	- **Target**: .NET Standard 2.0 / .NET Framework 4.6.1
54	- **IRCv3 support**: None documented. Supports standard IRC commands
55	  (JOIN, PART, PRIVMSG, NICK, KICK, QUIT, TOPIC) and CTCP.
56	- **Maintenance**: Low activity, 4 stars, 2 forks.
57	- **API quality**: Async/await first, observable collections for UI binding,
58	  fluent builder pattern for configuration, custom message handler system.
59	  The cleanest modern API of the candidates, but limited scope.
60	- **Verdict**: Interesting API design to draw inspiration from, but no IRCv3
61	  support makes it unsuitable as a dependency.
62	
63	#### IrcNet (NowaLone)
64	
65	- **Repository**: <https://github.com/NowaLone/IrcNet>
66	- **NuGet**: Multiple packages (`NowaLone.IrcNet.Parser.V3`,
67	  `NowaLone.IrcNet.Parser.Rfc1459`, `NowaLone.IrcNet.Client`, etc.)
68	- **License**: MIT
69	- **Target**: .NET Standard 2.0+, .NET Framework 4.6.1+, .NET 6+
70	- **IRCv3 support**: Has a dedicated V3 parser and V3 client extensions
71	  package. Specific IRCv3 features supported are not well-documented.
72	- **Maintenance**: Very new (first release Jan 2025, latest May 2025). Only
73	  1 star, 0 forks. Essentially a single-developer project with no community
74	  adoption.
75	- **API quality**: Well-structured package decomposition (abstractions,
76	  parsers, client, extensions). Uses `IrcClientWebSocket` with
77	  `OnMessageReceived` events and `SendAsync`. The modular package structure
78	  is appealing but the WebSocket-based client is unusual for IRC.
79	- **Verdict**: Too immature and unproven. The package decomposition is
80	  interesting for reference, but adopting a library with zero community
81	  adoption and unclear IRCv3 coverage is risky.
82	
83	### Recommendation
84	
85	**None of the existing C# IRC libraries are suitable** for Marv's
86	requirements. The landscape is fragmented between abandoned projects
87	targeting legacy .NET (ChatSharp, IRC.NET) and immature projects with
88	unclear IRCv3 coverage (IrcNet/NowaLone).
89	
90	**Recommended approach**: Build on a minimal IRC message parser (either our
91	own or adapted from the IRCv3-compliant parsing layer in IrcNet) and
92	implement connection management and capability negotiation ourselves. This
93	gives us:
94	
95	- Full control over IRCv3 capability negotiation
96	- Modern .NET 10 / C# 13 idioms (async/await, `System.Threading.Channels`)
97	- No dependency on unmaintained or immature libraries
98	- Ability to use the [ircdocs/parser-tests](https://github.com/ircdocs/parser-tests)
99	  test vectors to validate our parser
100	
101	The IRC protocol is text-based and relatively simple to parse correctly
102	(with known edge cases — see section 5). The hard part is capability
103	negotiation and state management, which none of the existing libraries
104	handle well enough anyway.
105	
106	---
107	
108	## 2. IRCv3 Capabilities Relevant to a Bot
109	
110	### Must-Have Capabilities
111	
112	| Capability | Why |
113	|---|---|
114	| `message-tags` | Foundation for all tag-based features. Allows receiving and sending arbitrary tags. |
115	| `server-time` | Accurate timestamps on messages. Essential for logging and event ordering. |
116	| `sasl` | Secure authentication to services (NickServ) during connection registration, before joining channels. Avoids race conditions with post-connect auth. |
117	| `multi-prefix` | Receive all prefix modes (e.g. `@+`) in NAMES/WHO, not just the highest. Required for accurate channel state tracking. |
118	| `account-tag` | Every message carries the sender's services account name. Enables account-based authorization in plugins without WHOIS queries. |
119	| `echo-message` | Server echoes our own PRIVMSGs/NOTICEs back. Essential for confirming message delivery and maintaining accurate channel logs. |
120	| `cap-notify` | Server notifies when capabilities become available/unavailable at runtime. Required for robust capability management. |
121	| `batch` | Groups related messages (e.g. WHOIS replies, netsplit/netjoin). Allows plugins to process related messages atomically. |
122	| `labeled-response` | Correlate sent commands with server responses. Critical for any command that expects a reply (e.g. WHO, WHOIS). Without this, responses from concurrent commands can be ambiguous. |
123	| `bot` (bot-mode) | Marks the bot with a `B` flag so users and servers know it is a bot. Increasingly required by networks. |
124	
125	### Should-Have Capabilities
126	
127	| Capability | Why |
128	|---|---|
129	| `account-notify` | Notified when users log in/out of services accounts. Keeps account-based auth state current without polling. |
130	| `away-notify` | Notified when users change away status. Useful for plugins that care about user presence. |
131	| `extended-join` | JOIN messages include the user's account name and realname. Eliminates WHOIS on join. |
132	| `invite-notify` | Notified when users are invited to channels the bot is in. |
133	| `userhost-in-names` | NAMES replies include full `nick!user@host` masks. Eliminates WHO queries for host info. |
134	| `chghost` | Notified when a user's host/ident changes (e.g. after cloaking). Keeps internal state accurate. |
135	| `setname` | Notified when a user changes their realname. |
136	| `standard-replies` | Servers can send standardized FAIL/WARN/NOTE responses. Better error handling. |
137	| `monitor` | Watch for specific nicks coming online/offline. Useful for admin notification plugins. |
138	
139	### Not Relevant for Marv
140	
141	| Capability | Why not |
142	|---|---|
143	| `chathistory` / `event-playback` | Marv explicitly does not handle historic messages. |
144	| `tls` (STARTTLS) | We will connect with TLS from the start, not upgrade. |
145	| `multiline` | Nice-to-have at best; most bot messages are short. |
146	| `read-marker` | Intended for multi-client users, not bots. |
147	| `message-redaction` | Not relevant for a bot that does not maintain chat history. |
148	| `metadata-2` | Not widely deployed yet. |
149	| `channel-rename` | Rare operation; basic handling sufficient. |
150	
151	### Important Tags
152	
153	| Tag | Purpose |
154	|---|---|
155	| `account` | Sender's account name (from `account-tag` cap). |
156	| `batch` | Batch identifier for grouped messages. |
157	| `bot` | Marks messages from bots. |
158	| `label` | Correlates requests with responses (from `labeled-response` cap). |
159	| `msgid` | Unique message ID assigned by the server. Useful for deduplication. |
160	| `time` | ISO 8601 timestamp (from `server-time` cap). |
161	| `+reply` | Client-to-client: marks a message as a reply to a specific `msgid`. |
162	
163	---
164	
165	## 3. Existing IRC Bot Architectures
166	
167	### Sopel (Python)
168	
169	Sopel is the most relevant reference for Marv's design, being a mature,
170	well-documented IRC bot framework with a strong plugin system.
171	
172	**Plugin structure**:
173	- Plugins are Python modules containing decorated callables.
174	- Optional lifecycle hooks: `setup()` (called before connection),
175	  `shutdown()` (called after disconnection), `configure()` (interactive
176	  setup wizard).
177	- Callables receive `(bot, trigger)` — the bot object for sending messages
178	  and accessing state, and the trigger containing the matched message.
179	
180	**Event handling**:
181	- Decorator-based: `@rule()` (regex match), `@command()` (named command),
182	  `@event()` (IRC event type), `@ctcp()` (CTCP messages).
183	- `@interval()` decorator for periodic tasks (no message trigger needed).
184	- Rate limiting decorators: `@rate()`, `@rate_user()`, `@rate_channel()`.
185	
186	**Inter-plugin communication**:
187	- `bot.memory` — runtime dict-like shared memory. Any plugin can read/write.
188	  No formal contracts or typing; just convention.
189	- `bot.db` — persistent SQLite database. Plugins share tables.
190	- No formal dependency declaration between plugins. Plugins assume
191	  shared state exists or check for it.
192	
193	**Access control**:
194	- Built-in decorators: `@require_admin()`, `@require_privilege()`,
195	  `@require_account()`, `@require_owner()`.
196	- Channel privilege integration for op/voice checks.
197	
198	**Strengths**: Very easy to write plugins. Good decorator API. Mature.
199	**Weaknesses**: No typed inter-plugin contracts. Shared memory is
200	convention-based and fragile. No way to declare "this plugin requires
201	that plugin." Synchronous architecture.
202	
203	### Limnoria / Supybot (Python)
204	
205	Fork of Supybot, the original extensible Python IRC bot.
206	
207	**Architecture**:
208	- Synchronous main loop (`drivers.run()` in a loop).
209	- Two driver types: Socket driver (IRC connection) and schedule driver
210	  (periodic tasks like cron).
211	- All messages flow through `irclib.Irc`, which maintains a callback
212	  registry of plugins.
213	
214	**Plugin dispatch**:
215	- Uses `IrcCommandDispatcher` — method dispatch based on IRC command name
216	  (e.g., `doTopic()` handles TOPIC messages, `doPrivmsg()` handles PRIVMSG).
217	- `inFilter` / `outFilter` methods on plugins intercept messages before
218	  receipt / before sending.
219	
220	**State management**:
221	- Plugins maintain their own state. No shared memory system like Sopel.
222	- Each plugin can have its own database.
223	
224	**Strengths**: Well-tested, huge plugin ecosystem, granular ACL system.
225	**Weaknesses**: Synchronous, complex callback system, steeper learning
226	curve than Sopel, no typed inter-plugin services.
227	
228	### Errbot (Python, multi-platform)
229	
230	A chatbot framework supporting Slack, IRC, XMPP, Telegram, and more.
231	
232	**Architecture**:
233	- Backend abstraction: each chat platform is a "backend" implementing a
234	  common interface. Plugins are platform-agnostic.
235	- Plugins inherit from `BotPlugin` and use `@botcmd` decorator.
236	- Built-in persistent storage: `self['key'] = value` (dict-like).
237	- Plugin management via chat commands (!repos install, !plugin enable).
238	
239	**Relevance to Marv**: Errbot's backend abstraction is interesting as a
240	reference for how to separate protocol handling from plugin logic, even
241	though Marv only targets IRC. The `BotPlugin` base class with built-in
242	storage and command decorators is a good DX pattern.
243	
244	### Chaskis (C#, .NET)
245	
246	The only C# IRC bot framework found. Uses a plugin-based architecture.
247	
248	- Last release: v0.31.0 (January 2021) — appears abandoned.
249	- Targets .NET Core.
250	- Plugin-based, but detailed architecture documentation is sparse.
251	- No evidence of IRCv3 support.
252	
253	**Relevance**: Demonstrates that C# IRC bot frameworks exist but confirms
254	the ecosystem is immature. The wiki (if still accessible) may have useful
255	patterns.
256	
257	### Key Design Patterns Across Frameworks
258	
259	1. **Decorator/attribute-based event registration**: Every modern framework
260	   uses decorators (Python) or attributes (C#) to declare what events a
261	   handler responds to. This is clearly the right pattern.
262	
263	2. **Bot object as service facade**: Plugins receive a `bot` object that
264	   provides sending capabilities and state access. This keeps plugins
265	   decoupled from protocol details.
266	
267	3. **Configuration via bot object (anti-pattern)**: All studied frameworks
268	   have plugins access configuration through the bot object (e.g.
269	   `bot.config`, `self.config` from a base class). This couples plugins
270	   to the framework's configuration plumbing and makes configuration
271	   requirements implicit. **For Marv, we will not follow this pattern.**
272	   Instead, plugins will declare a typed configuration class, which will
273	   be injected via constructor — making configuration requirements
274	   explicit, validated at startup, and testable without a bot instance.
275	
276	4. **Trigger/context object**: The matched message, parsed into a
277	   convenient form with sender info, channel, match groups, etc.
278	
279	5. **Lifecycle hooks**: setup/teardown at plugin level, tied to bot
280	   lifecycle rather than individual messages.
281	
282	6. **Missing pattern**: None of the frameworks studied have typed
283	   inter-plugin service contracts. They all use shared mutable state
284	   (dicts, databases) or assume co-residency. This is Marv's opportunity
285	   to do better.
286	
287	---
288	
289	## 4. Plugin Architectures with Inter-Plugin Services
290	
291	### The Problem
292	
293	We want plugins to be able to:
294	1. **Provide services** (e.g., an auth plugin exposes `IAuthorizationService`)
295	2. **Consume services** from other plugins (e.g., a moderation plugin uses
296	   `IAuthorizationService` to check permissions)
297	3. **Handle optional dependencies** gracefully (the moderation plugin works
298	   without auth, just with reduced functionality)
299	4. **Load in correct order** based on dependencies
300	
301	### Approach 1: .NET `IServiceProvider` (Microsoft DI)
302	
303	**How it works**: Plugins register services in a `IServiceCollection`
304	during a configuration phase. The DI container is built once. Plugins
305	receive dependencies via constructor injection.
306	
307	**Pros**:
308	- Standard .NET pattern; familiar to C# developers
309	- Well-tested, high-performance implementation
310	- Supports scoped, singleton, and transient lifetimes
311	- Constructor injection makes dependencies explicit and testable
312	
313	**Cons**:
314	- Container is built once and is immutable — adding services after build
315	  requires rebuilding the container
316	- Plugin load order must be determined before container build
317	- Optional dependencies are awkward (you must register a null/no-op
318	  implementation or use `IServiceProvider.GetService<T>()` which returns
319	  null)
320	- No built-in concept of "this service is provided by plugin X"
321	
322	**Suitability**: Good foundation, but needs augmentation for plugin
323	lifecycle management.
324	
325	### Approach 2: Explicit Service Registry
326	
327	**How it works**: A custom `IServiceRegistry` managed by the bot core.
328	Plugins call `registry.Register<IAuthService>(this, implementation)` during
329	setup and `registry.Get<IAuthService>()` to consume. The registry
330	understands plugin identity and lifecycle.
331	
332	**Pros**:
333	- Can track which plugin provides which service
334	- Can handle late registration and optional dependencies naturally
335	- Can provide diagnostics ("auth service provided by AuthPlugin v1.2")
336	- Can enforce single-provider-per-interface or allow multiple providers
337	
338	**Cons**:
339	- Service locator anti-pattern (hides dependencies)
340	- Must be built and maintained ourselves
341	- Less familiar to .NET developers
342	- Testing requires mocking the registry
343	
344	### Recommended Approach: Hybrid
345	
346	Use Microsoft's `IServiceCollection` / `IServiceProvider` as the underlying
347	mechanism, but wrap it in a plugin-aware layer. **There is a single DI
348	container for the entire application** — core services (configuration,
349	logging, IRC client, bot) and plugin-contributed services all live in one
350	`IServiceCollection` which builds one `IServiceProvider`. There is no
351	separate container for plugins.
352	
353	The startup sequence has two distinct phases:
354	
355	1. **Bootstrap phase** (before DI): Read configuration from files,
356	   environment variables, and command-line arguments. Determine which
357	   plugin assemblies to load. This phase uses no DI — it runs before the
358	   container exists. The plugin list comes from core configuration read
359	   during bootstrap, so there is no circular dependency.
360	
361	2. **DI phase** (building the single container):
362	   a. **Assembly loading**: Load plugin assemblies discovered during
363	      bootstrap, discover plugin types.
364	   b. **Dependency sort**: Topological sort based on declared dependencies
365	      (`[DependsOn(typeof(AuthPlugin))]` attributes on plugin classes)
366	      and constructor parameter inspection (non-core types are inferred
367	      as service dependencies; nullable parameters with a default of
368	      `null` are treated as optional).
369	   c. **Service registration**: Each plugin gets a chance to register
370	      services into the shared `IServiceCollection` via a static or
371	      type-level method (e.g. `ConfigureServices(IServiceCollection)`).
372	      This does not require constructing the plugin. Core services
373	      (configuration, logging, IRC client) are also registered here.
374	      Plugins are called in dependency order.
375	   d. **Container build**: The single `IServiceProvider` is built.
376	   e. **Plugin construction**: Plugin instances are resolved from the
377	      container — configuration and services injected via constructor.
378	
379	3. **Runtime**: Plugins can query for optional services via
380	   `IServiceProvider.GetService<T>()` (returns null if not registered).
381	
382	This gives us:
383	- A single, standard .NET DI container for the whole application
384	- Familiar .NET DI patterns for most use cases
385	- Explicit dependency declarations for load ordering
386	- Optional dependencies via nullable injection or runtime resolution
387	- Plugin identity tracking via a thin metadata layer on top
388	
389	### Load Order and Optional Dependencies
390	
391	**Topological sort**: Declare dependencies as attributes. Before loading,
392	build a dependency graph and topologically sort it. Cycles are a
393	configuration error. Missing required dependencies prevent startup.
394	Optional dependencies are ordered-if-present but don't block.
395	
396	**Graceful degradation**: Plugins should declare whether a service
397	dependency is required or optional. Required = fail to load if not met.
398	Optional = load but potentially with reduced functionality. Optional
399	dependencies are expressed as nullable constructor parameters with a
400	default of `null`.
401	
402	---
403	
404	## 5. Common Failure Modes in IRC Bot Implementations
405	
406	Based on the [ircdocs/parser-tests](https://github.com/ircdocs/parser-tests)
407	test suite, the [Modern IRC documentation](https://modern.ircdocs.horse/),
408	implementation guides, and issue trackers of IRC libraries, the following
409	edge cases and failure modes are most common.
410	
411	### Message Parsing
412	
413	1. **Trailing parameter not treated as normal parameter**: Many parsers
414	   create a separate field for the trailing parameter (after `:`). The
415	   correct behavior is to always add it to the parameter list as a normal
416	   parameter. Creating a separate `trailing` field leads to bugs when
417	   commands have varying parameter counts.
418	
419	2. **Tags on unexpected messages**: Once `message-tags` or `server-time` is
420	   enabled, ANY message from the server can have tags. Parsers that only
421	   handle tags on PRIVMSG will break on tagged CAP, AUTHENTICATE, JOIN,
422	   etc.
423	
424	3. **Tag value escaping**: Backslash escaping in tag values follows specific
425	   rules (`\:` → `;`, `\s` → space, `\\` → `\`, `\r` → CR, `\n` → LF).
426	   A trailing backslash with no escape character should produce no output
427	   character. An invalid escape like `\b` should drop the backslash
428	   (producing `b`).
429	
430	4. **Multiple spaces between parameters**: The spec says parameters are
431	   separated by "one or more" spaces. Parsers that split on single space
432	   will produce empty parameters. Parsers that are too strict will reject
433	   valid messages.
434	
435	5. **Empty trailing parameter**: `:` with nothing after it is a valid empty
436	   trailing parameter, not a parse error.
437	
438	6. **Source (prefix) parsing**: The source prefix `nick!user@host` can have
439	   missing components (server-originated messages have no `!user@host`).
440	   Parsers must handle `nick`, `nick@host`, `nick!user@host`, and bare
441	   server names.
442	
443	### Case Mapping
444	
445	7. **CASEMAPPING inconsistency**: Servers advertise their case mapping via
446	   ISUPPORT (`CASEMAPPING=rfc1459|ascii|strict-rfc1459`). Under `rfc1459`,
447	   `{`, `|`, `}` are the lowercase equivalents of `[`, `\`, `]`. Under
448	   `strict-rfc1459`, `~` and `^` are additionally equivalent. Under `ascii`,
449	   only A-Z/a-z are equivalent. Bots that use standard `ToLower()` for nick
450	   comparison will have subtle bugs. **This must be handled correctly for
451	   all nick and channel name comparisons**.
452	
453	8. **CASEMAPPING can change**: The server sends ISUPPORT during registration,
454	   but it can technically resend it. The bot should use the server's
455	   advertised mapping, not a hardcoded default.
456	
457	### Connection Management
458	
459	9. **PING/PONG timeout**: Servers send PING periodically; failing to PONG
460	   within the timeout causes disconnection ("Ping timeout"). Bots must
461	   handle PING at the protocol level, not in a plugin.
462	
463	10. **Excess flood**: Sending too many messages too quickly causes the server
464	    to disconnect the client ("Excess Flood"). Bots need rate limiting /
465	    message queue with throttling. A common approach is a token bucket or
466	    leaky bucket algorithm.
467	
468	11. **Reconnection with backoff**: Network disruptions are common. Bots
469	    should reconnect with exponential backoff to avoid being banned for
470	    rapid reconnection. Some networks also require different nick on
471	    reconnection if the old session hasn't timed out (nick collision).
472	
473	12. **Registration race conditions**: Without SASL, authenticating to
474	    NickServ after connecting creates a window where the bot is
475	    unauthenticated. During this window, joining channels with `+r`
476	    (registered-only) will fail. SASL solves this.
477	
478	### Channel and User State
479	
480	13. **MODE parsing complexity**: Channel modes have varying parameter counts
481	    depending on the mode type (A/B/C/D as defined in ISUPPORT
482	    `CHANMODES`). Parsing `MODE #channel +ov nick1 nick2` requires knowing
483	    that `o` and `v` are type B (always have a parameter). Getting this
484	    wrong causes state tracking to desynchronize.
485	
486	14. **NAMES/WHO state initialization**: When joining a channel, the bot must
487	    process NAMES (RPL_NAMREPLY/RPL_ENDOFNAMES) to build the initial user
488	    list. With `multi-prefix`, each name can have multiple prefix
489	    characters. Without `userhost-in-names`, the bot only gets nicks (no
490	    hostmasks).
491	
492	15. **Nick changes affecting state**: When a user changes nick, all channel
493	    membership records must be updated. This is a common source of state
494	    desynchronization.
495	
496	16. **QUIT vs PART**: QUIT removes a user from all channels; PART removes
497	    from one. Bots that only handle PART will have stale user lists.
498	
499	### Encoding
500	
501	17. **Character encoding**: IRC has no standard encoding. Messages can be
502	    UTF-8, Latin-1, or arbitrary bytes. Bots should default to UTF-8 but
503	    handle invalid sequences gracefully (replace or skip, never crash).
504	
505	### Protocol Quirks
506	
507	18. **Numeric replies are strings**: Despite being called "numerics," IRC
508	    reply codes are transmitted as three-character strings (e.g., `"001"`),
509	    not integers. Leading zeros matter.
510	
511	19. **Maximum message length**: IRC messages are limited to 512 bytes
512	    (including CR-LF), or 8191 bytes with the `message-tags` spec (tags
513	    occupy a separate 8191-byte budget from the 512-byte message budget).
514	    Bots must split messages that exceed these limits.
515	
516	20. **CAP negotiation subtleties**: `CAP LS 302` (IRCv3.2) enables
517	    multi-line capability lists and capability values. Bots must handle
518	    both single-line (`CAP * LS :cap1 cap2`) and multi-line
519	    (`CAP * LS * :cap1 cap2` followed by `CAP * LS :cap3`) responses.
520	    The `*` indicates "more to follow."
521	
522	### Test Resources
523	
524	- **Parser test vectors**: <https://github.com/ircdocs/parser-tests> —
525	  YAML test files covering message splitting, tag parsing, mask matching,
526	  and hostname validation. Tests aggregated from Mozilla, grawity, and
527	  community contributions.
528	- **Modern IRC docs**: <https://modern.ircdocs.horse/> — living document
529	  describing the IRC protocol as actually implemented (as opposed to the
530	  dated RFCs).
531	- **Implementation guide**: <https://modern.ircdocs.horse/impl.html> —
532	  specifically addresses common implementation mistakes.
533

1	# Prompt Log
2	
3	## Research the problem space before writing code
4	
5	**Date**: 2026-05-30T00:00:00Z
6	
7	**Prompt**:
8	
9	> Before anything else, please read CLAUDE.md and acknowledge the prompt logging and git requirements
10	>
11	> Before we write any code, I want you to research the problem space.
12	> Please investigate the following and produce a research summary saved
13	> to docs/research.md:
14	>
15	> 1. Existing C# IRC libraries available on NuGet — evaluate candidates on:
16	>    - IRCv3 support, including message tags
17	>    - Quality of the API (is it easy to build on top of?)
18	>    - Maintenance status and test coverage
19	>    - Licensing
20	>    Make a recommendation with justification. We will use one of these
21	>    rather than implementing the IRC protocol from scratch.
22	>
23	> 2. IRCv3 specifically:
24	>    - What capabilities are most relevant to a bot (message tags,
25	>      account-tag, labeled-response, etc)?
26	>
27	> 3. Existing IRC bots — look at how bots/frameworks that
28	>    support multiple platforms design their internal models and APIs.
29	>    Consider IRC bots in multiple languages, and pay particular attention to
30	>    Sopel.
31	>
32	> 4. Plugin architectures with inter-plugin services — we want plugins
33	>    to be able to register services (e.g. an authn/authz service) that
34	>    other plugins can consume. Research:
35	>    - How existing bot frameworks handle inter-plugin dependencies
36	>    - Whether .NET's IServiceProvider pattern is appropriate here, or
37	>      whether a more explicit service registry is better
38	>    - How to handle load order and optional dependencies gracefully
39	>
40	> 5. Common failure modes in IRC bot implementations — from test suites
41	>    and issue trackers, what edge cases trip up IRC clients most often?
42	>    List them — we will use this to inform our test suite.
43	>
44	> Do not write any C# code yet. The deliverable is docs/research.md only.
45	
46	## Plugin configuration should be injected, not accessed via bot object
47	
48	**Date**: 2026-05-30T00:00:00Z
49	
50	**Prompt**:
51	
52	> In the conclusion of section 3, you note that it is common for plugins to access configuration from a bot object. I consider this to be an anti-pattern; for Marv, plugins should declare a class modeling their configuration, which will be injected into the plugin when it's constructed.
53	
54	## Question about plugin loading and configuration viability

56	**Date**: 2026-05-30T00:00:00Z

72	## Produce design documents for the bot
73	
74	**Date**: 2026-05-30T09:30:00Z

76	**Prompt**:
77	
78	> Before anything else, please read CLAUDE.md and acknowledge the prompt logging and git requirements.
79	>
80	> Read docs/research.md and produce a design for the bot. Deliverables:
81	>
82	> 1. docs/architecture.md covering:
83	>    - Project/assembly structure and responsibilities
84	>    - The layering of components, parsers, etc
85	>    - The async/threading model
86	>    - How plugins are discovered and loaded at runtime
87	>    - How inter-plugin services work: registration, discovery, optional
88	>      vs required dependencies, and load order
89	>
90	> 2. docs/platform-abstraction-draft.md — describe the core concepts that
91	>    the bot will model and present to plugins:
92	>    - Messages (including metadata/tags)
93	>    - Channels and users
94	>    - Capabilities (how plugins discover what the current platform
95	>      supports — e.g. not all platforms have message editing)
96	>    - Events (message received, user joined, etc)
97	>
98	> 3. docs/plugin-api-draft.md covering:
99	>    - What types a plugin author works with day-to-day
100	>    - How a plugin registers interest in events
101	>    - How a plugin registers a service for other plugins to consume
102	>    - How a plugin declares and resolves a dependency on another
103	>      plugin's service
104	>    - What the bot exposes back to plugins (sending messages, querying
105	>      channel/user state, etc)
106	>    - A concrete example: what does the simplest possible plugin look
107	>      like, and what does a plugin that depends on an auth service look
108	>      like?
109	>
110	> 4. ADRs in docs/adr/:
111	>    - 001-platform-abstraction.md — how and why we model the IRC network's
112	>      entities
113	>    - 002-irc-library-choice.md — which IRC library we use and why
114	>      (from the research recommendation)
115	>    - 003-plugin-service-registry.md — how inter-plugin services work
116	>    - 004-async-model.md — the async/threading model
117	>
118	>> Do not write any C# code yet. I will review these documents and
119	> approve them before we proceed to implementation.

121	## Review feedback on design documents
122	
123	**Date**: 2026-05-30T10:00:00Z
124	
125	**Prompt**:
126	
127	> Some questions and notes regarding the architecture document:
128	>
129	> - Should Marv.App be the one building the DI container? Plugin loading belongs in the core, which seems like a conflict.
130	> - Is a single IrcMessage class for both inbound and outbound messages suitable?
131	> - Handling PING/PONG at the connection layer seems like it breaks layering, why not handle it in the core?
132	> - I'd prefer to have each plugin run simultaneously - i.e. they should each have their own task and channel to receive IrcMessages. Is this feasible while still allowing direct access to the channel/user stores?
133	> - The ProvidesService and ConsumesService attributes aren't great DX - can we gather this info automatically?
134	> - Requiring plugins to register their own config classes isn't great DX either - I anticipate that most plugins will not register services, but will have configs. Can we automate this, potentially through the abstract plugin base class?
135	>
136	> Some questions and notes regarding the platform-abstraction-draft:
137	>
138	> - Do we need the IChannelMember relation? There are really only two properties (Prefixes, JoinedAt) - all others can be derived from them. Might make more sense to store them in the IChannel itself
139	> - Can we combine the Channel*Event and Private*Events into a common class with a flag like IsDirect?
140	> - I'm not sure whether we really want to handle BatchEvent - this forces plugin authors to handle events both by themselves and as batched. Is there a simple way to let authors choose to un-batch them?
141	>
142	> Some questions and notes regarding plugin-api-draft.md:
143	>
144	> - What is the purpose of having both IBot.GetChannel and IBot.Channels? If there's not a good reason, I prefer just having IBot.Channels.
145	> - Why is there no IBot.Users, only IBot.GetUser? For consistency there should be IBot.Users. If there's not a good reason, we should also get rid of IBot.GetUser.
146	> - What does IBot.SendAndAwaitAsync do?
147	> - In addition to the OnCommand attribute, I'd like an OnRegex attribute which matches against a regular expression and passes the handler the Match object in it's context.
148	> - In the plugin structure, you mention that handlers may be in their own namespace/classes, but the only examples are of handlers in the main plugin class. How would this work?
149

157	
158	> let's go with the attribute approach, update the docs

172	## Responses to design documentation sanity check
173	
174	**Date**: 2026-05-30T11:07:00Z
175	
176	**Prompt**:
177	
178	> Responses to the sanity check:
179	>
180	> - Remove the dangling references to ConsumeService
181	> - Regarding the IUser mutation model, can you sketch out what atomic property replacement would look like?
182	> - Regarding IChannel.SendMessageAsync, change the eaxmples to use bot.SendMessageAsync instead.
183	> - Regarding CTCP events, implement CTCP VERSION, PING and TIME in the core, but also provide a generic CtcpEvent to allow plugins to implement arbritrary commands. Note that the internal implementations of CTCP commands must not expose host information, only bot version, etc.
184	> - Regarding plugin configuration, instead of selecting the plugin config class through MarvPlugin<TConfig>, scan the assembly for classes tagged with a [PluginConfig(Section = "Something")] attribute and register that accordingly.
185	> - The configuration file format should default to JSON, but there should also be a CLI argument to specify the config path; if this is provided, the extension of the config path should determine the file format.
186	> - For command-line handling, use System.CommandLine 2.x
187	> - Regarding reconnection behaviour, the default stance should be for state to be discarded - SendAndAwaitAsync calls should be cancelled, message queues cleared, and stale objects discarded.
188	> - Handler discovery rules: Handler methods don't need to be public. The dispatching should be done from within MarvPlugin so that protected methods are accessible. This does raise the question of how MarvPlugin can construct the HandlerGroup handlers, though.
189	>   If multiple handlers have the same OnEvent, they will be called consecutively but in an undefined order.
190	>   HandlerGroups may handle all events, including lifecycle events.
191	> - Remove the references to HasService<T> and GetOptionalService<T>.
192	> - I think it's reasonable to inject IBot into plugins by default, unless you can think of a good reason not to?
193	
194	## Further design refinements
195	
196	**Date**: 2026-05-30T11:32:00Z
197	
198	**Prompt**:
199	
200	> Some more thoughts:
201	>
202	> - If optional dependencies are already nullable constructor parameters, do we need OptionalService?
203	> - Document that assemblies may only contain one MarvPlugin, and it must contain a static property declaring it's name, to be used in log messages, the plugin loading config, etc.
204	> - I'm not sure I like the snapshot-on-publish approach - what are the risks involved with allowing the models to be mutable, and is there a way to mitigate those risks?
205	> - Note that handler methods inside MarvPlugin don't need to be public, but those in HandlerGroups do.
206	> - How will IBot injection into GreetPlugin be accomplished? I expected a constructor parameter that's passed to the base MarvPlugin constructor.
207	
208	## Mutable models confirmed, enforce PluginName via interface
209	
210	**Date**: 2026-05-30T11:43:00Z
211	
212	**Prompt**:
213	
214	> I'm okay with the mutable model approach, so long as it's clearly documented.
215	>
216	> Instead of validating PluginName at discovery time, enforce it with an interface. In fact, include all the plugin methods on that interface so that authors can bypass MarvPlugin if they want.
217	
218	## Make event dispatch explicit via HandleEventAsync
219	
220	**Date**: 2026-05-30T11:59:00Z
221	
222	**Prompt**:
223	
224	> It's not very clear how handlers are actually called at this point - I think the assumption is that the core directly calls the handler, which feels too magical to me.
225	> What I'd like to see is an OnIrcEvent method on IPlugin (feel free to change the name to something more appropriate), with the implementation in MarvPlugin using reflection to discover handlers and dispatch the events accordingly.
226	> MarvPlugin will also need to be provided with a way to instantiate classes, to dispatch to HandlerGroup classes. My first thought is a utility class which wraps IServiceProvider and uses ActivatorUtilities.CreateInstance<T>, but I'm open to other possibilities.
227	
228	## Make IPluginActivator.CreateInstance generic
229	
230	**Date**: 2026-05-30T12:06:00Z
231	
232	**Prompt**:
233	
234	> That looks good, except that the IPluginActivator should make CreateInstance generic, i.e. T CreateInstance<T>(params object[] params);
235	
236	## ConfigureServices on IPlugin, reinstantiate on reconnect
237	
238	**Date**: 2026-05-30T12:21:00Z
239	
240	**Prompt**:
241	
242	> Make ConfigureServices a static method on IPlugin, with an empty default implementation.
243	> Don't register the plugins or HandlerGroups themselves in the service provider. Instantiate them with ActivatorUtilities instead. Plugins can be reinstantiated when reconnecting to a server so that there's no chance of stale references.
244	> Update architecture.md to note that the core should not call handlers on HandlerGroups; MarvPlugin will do that.

> Proceed in this order, verifying that each layer compiles and its
> tests pass before moving to the next:
>
> 1. Solution and project structure (csproj/sln files only, no logic)
>
> 2. The platform abstraction layer — the interfaces and types that
>    define channels, users, messages, metadata/tags, events, and
>    capabilities. No implementation yet, just contracts.
>
> 3. The IRC layer, including the parser, capability manager and interface implementations.
>    Unit test edge cases identified in docs/research.md. Include references to sources such as `ircdocs/parser-tests`.
>
> 4. The plugin host — plugin discovery/loading, the event dispatch
>    pipeline, and utility classes as designed in ADR 003.
>
> 5. The plugin API project — the types plugin authors reference including
>    the implementation of MarvPlugin.
>
> 6. Four reference plugins to validate the API:
>    a. A simple greeting plugin — to validate basic event handling and message sending
>    b. A two-plugin pair: one plugin that registers an auth service, and one
>       that consumes it — to validate the inter-plugin service mechanism
>    c. A plugin demonstrating use of HandlerGroups to return canned responses
>
> 7. The main application - include support for JSON, YAML, XML and TOML configuration.
>
> Provide an option (makefile or build steps) to copy all the assemblies and plugins to one directory ready for use.
>
> Rules:
> - Run `dotnet build` after each layer. Fix all errors before
>   continuing.
> - Run `dotnet test` after each layer. Fix all failures before
>   continuing.
> - Commit your changes to git after each layer.
> - If you make any architectural decision not covered by the ADRs,
>   write a new ADR before writing the code that depends on it.
> - Do not change the plugin API or platform abstraction surface without
>   flagging it to me first.

📝 Write MessageSource.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Protocol/MessageSource.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Protocol;

/// <summary>
/// Represents the source (prefix) of an IRC message, parsed into its components.
/// Server-originated messages may have only a <see cref="Host"/> with no nick or user.
/// </summary>
public sealed class MessageSource
{
    /// <summary>The nickname component, if present.</summary>
    public string? Nick { get; }

    /// <summary>The username (ident) component, if present.</summary>
    public string? User { get; }

    /// <summary>The hostname component, if present.</summary>
    public string? Host { get; }

    /// <summary>
    /// Creates a new <see cref="MessageSource"/> with the specified components.
    /// </summary>
    public MessageSource(string? nick, string? user, string? host)
    {
        Nick = nick;
        User = user;
        Host = host;
    }

    /// <summary>
    /// Returns the full hostmask representation (nick!user@host),
    /// omitting missing components.
    /// </summary>
    public override string ToString()
    {
        if (Nick is null)
            return Host ?? "";

        var result = Nick;
        if (User is not null)
            result += "!" + User;
        if (Host is not null)
            result += "@" + Host;
        return result;
    }
}

📝 Write IrcMessage.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Protocol/IrcMessage.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Protocol;

/// <summary>
/// Immutable representation of an IRC message, used for both inbound and outbound messages.
/// The trailing parameter (after ':') is always folded into <see cref="Parameters"/>
/// as the last element — there is no separate trailing property.
/// </summary>
public sealed class IrcMessage
{
    /// <summary>IRCv3 message tags, with values already unescaped.</summary>
    public IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string?> Tags { get; }

    /// <summary>
    /// The message source (nick!user@host prefix). Null for outbound messages.
    /// </summary>
    public MessageSource? Source { get; }

    /// <summary>The IRC command, always uppercase (e.g. "PRIVMSG", "001", "CAP").</summary>
    public string Command { get; }

    /// <summary>The message parameters, including any trailing parameter as the last element.</summary>
    public IReadOnlyList<string> Parameters { get; }

    /// <summary>
    /// Creates a new IRC message with all components.
    /// </summary>
    public IrcMessage(
        IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string?>? tags,
        MessageSource? source,
        string command,
        IReadOnlyList<string> parameters)
    {
        Tags = tags ?? EmptyTags;
        Source = source;
        Command = command.ToUpperInvariant();
        Parameters = parameters;
    }

    /// <summary>
    /// Creates a new outbound IRC message (no source, no tags).
    /// </summary>
    public IrcMessage(string command, IReadOnlyList<string> parameters)
        : this(null, null, command, parameters)
    {
    }

    /// <summary>
    /// Creates a new outbound IRC message with tags (no source).
    /// </summary>
    public IrcMessage(IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string?> tags, string command, IReadOnlyList<string> parameters)
        : this(tags, null, command, parameters)
    {
    }

    private static readonly IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string?> EmptyTags =
        new Dictionary<string, string?>().AsReadOnly();
}

📝 Write CaseMapping.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Protocol/CaseMapping.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Protocol;

/// <summary>
/// IRC case mapping rules as defined by the CASEMAPPING ISUPPORT token.
/// Determines how nicknames and channel names are compared.
/// </summary>
public enum CaseMappingType
{
    /// <summary>
    /// RFC 1459 case mapping: A-Z maps to a-z, and additionally
    /// [ \ ] map to { | } respectively.
    /// </summary>
    Rfc1459,

    /// <summary>
    /// Strict RFC 1459 case mapping: same as RFC 1459 but also
    /// ^ maps to ~.
    /// </summary>
    StrictRfc1459,

    /// <summary>
    /// ASCII case mapping: only A-Z maps to a-z. No special character mappings.
    /// </summary>
    Ascii
}

/// <summary>
/// Provides case-folding and comparison utilities for IRC nicknames and channel names,
/// respecting the server's advertised CASEMAPPING.
/// </summary>
public static class CaseMapping
{
    /// <summary>
    /// Converts a character to its lowercase equivalent under the specified case mapping.
    /// </summary>
    public static char ToLower(char c, CaseMappingType mapping)
    {
        if (c >= 'A' && c <= 'Z')
            return (char)(c + 32);

        return mapping switch
        {
            CaseMappingType.Rfc1459 => c switch
            {
                '[' => '{',
                '\\' => '|',
                ']' => '}',
                _ => c
            },
            CaseMappingType.StrictRfc1459 => c switch
            {
                '[' => '{',
                '\\' => '|',
                ']' => '}',
                '^' => '~',
                _ => c
            },
            _ => c
        };
    }

    /// <summary>
    /// Folds a string to lowercase under the specified case mapping.
    /// </summary>
    public static string Fold(string value, CaseMappingType mapping)
    {
        var chars = new char[value.Length];
        for (var i = 0; i < value.Length; i++)
            chars[i] = ToLower(value[i], mapping);
        return new string(chars);
    }

    /// <summary>
    /// Compares two strings for equality under the specified case mapping.
    /// </summary>
    public static bool AreEqual(string a, string b, CaseMappingType mapping)
    {
        if (a.Length != b.Length)
            return false;

        for (var i = 0; i < a.Length; i++)
        {
            if (ToLower(a[i], mapping) != ToLower(b[i], mapping))
                return false;
        }

        return true;
    }

    /// <summary>
    /// Creates an <see cref="IEqualityComparer{T}"/> for strings using the specified case mapping.
    /// </summary>
    public static IEqualityComparer<string> GetComparer(CaseMappingType mapping) =>
        new IrcCaseComparer(mapping);

    private sealed class IrcCaseComparer(CaseMappingType mapping) : IEqualityComparer<string>
    {
        public bool Equals(string? x, string? y)
        {
            if (ReferenceEquals(x, y)) return true;
            if (x is null || y is null) return false;
            return AreEqual(x, y, mapping);
        }

        public int GetHashCode(string obj)
        {
            var hash = new HashCode();
            foreach (var c in obj)
                hash.Add(ToLower(c, mapping));
            return hash.ToHashCode();
        }
    }
}

📝 Write IUser.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Platform/IUser.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Platform;

/// <summary>
/// Represents a user the bot is aware of through shared channels or direct interaction.
/// Properties are mutable — the message processor updates them in place when state changes
/// occur (NICK, CHGHOST, AWAY, etc.). Individual property reads are atomic; cross-property
/// consistency is not guaranteed during a handler.
/// </summary>
public interface IUser
{
    /// <summary>The user's current nickname.</summary>
    string Nick { get; }

    /// <summary>The user's ident/username, if known.</summary>
    string? User { get; }

    /// <summary>The user's hostname, if known.</summary>
    string? Host { get; }

    /// <summary>The user's services account name, if known (from account-tag, extended-join, or WHOX).</summary>
    string? Account { get; }

    /// <summary>The user's real name (GECOS), if known (from extended-join or WHOIS).</summary>
    string? RealName { get; }

    /// <summary>Whether the user is currently marked as away (from away-notify).</summary>
    bool IsAway { get; }

    /// <summary>The user's away message, if away.</summary>
    string? AwayMessage { get; }

    /// <summary>Whether the user is flagged as a bot (from bot tag or bot mode).</summary>
    bool IsBot { get; }

    /// <summary>The channels this user shares with the bot.</summary>
    IReadOnlyCollection<IChannel> Channels { get; }

    /// <summary>The full hostmask in nick!user@host format.</summary>
    string Hostmask { get; }
}

📝 Write IChannel.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Platform/IChannel.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Platform;

/// <summary>
/// Represents a channel the bot is currently a member of.
/// Properties are mutable — the message processor updates them in place.
/// Collection properties use concurrent-safe data structures for safe enumeration.
/// </summary>
public interface IChannel
{
    /// <summary>The channel name (e.g. "#channel").</summary>
    string Name { get; }

    /// <summary>The channel topic, if set.</summary>
    string? Topic { get; }

    /// <summary>The nick or mask that set the current topic.</summary>
    string? TopicSetBy { get; }

    /// <summary>When the current topic was set.</summary>
    DateTimeOffset? TopicSetAt { get; }

    /// <summary>The channel's modes and their parameters (mode char → parameter or null).</summary>
    IReadOnlyDictionary<char, string?> Modes { get; }

    /// <summary>The users currently in this channel.</summary>
    IReadOnlyCollection<IUser> Members { get; }

    /// <summary>Gets the prefix modes (e.g. '@', '+') for a user in this channel.</summary>
    IReadOnlySet<char> GetPrefixes(string nick);

    /// <summary>Gets when a user joined this channel, if known.</summary>
    DateTimeOffset? GetJoinTime(string nick);

    /// <summary>Returns whether the specified nick is a member of this channel.</summary>
    bool HasMember(string nick);

    /// <summary>Returns whether the specified nick has operator status ('@') in this channel.</summary>
    bool IsOp(string nick);

    /// <summary>Returns whether the specified nick has voice status ('+') in this channel.</summary>
    bool IsVoiced(string nick);

    /// <summary>When this channel was created, if known (from RPL_CREATIONTIME).</summary>
    DateTimeOffset? CreatedAt { get; }
}

📝 Write ICapabilityManager.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Platform/ICapabilityManager.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Platform;

/// <summary>
/// Manages IRCv3 capability negotiation state. Plugins can query which capabilities
/// are available and which have been successfully negotiated.
/// </summary>
public interface ICapabilityManager
{
    /// <summary>Returns whether the specified capability has been successfully negotiated.</summary>
    bool IsNegotiated(string capability);

    /// <summary>Returns whether the server advertises the specified capability.</summary>
    bool IsAvailable(string capability);

    /// <summary>The set of capabilities that have been successfully negotiated.</summary>
    IReadOnlySet<string> NegotiatedCapabilities { get; }

    /// <summary>
    /// All capabilities the server advertises, with their values (null if no value).
    /// </summary>
    IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string?> AvailableCapabilities { get; }

    /// <summary>Raised when capabilities change at runtime (from cap-notify).</summary>
    event EventHandler? CapabilitiesChanged;
}

📝 Write IServerInfo.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Platform/IServerInfo.cs
using Marv.Core.Protocol;

namespace Marv.Core.Platform;

/// <summary>
/// Provides access to server configuration advertised through ISUPPORT (005) numerics.
/// Updated live as the server sends ISUPPORT messages.
/// </summary>
public interface IServerInfo
{
    /// <summary>The network name, if advertised.</summary>
    string? NetworkName { get; }

    /// <summary>The case mapping used by this server for nick/channel comparisons.</summary>
    CaseMappingType CaseMapping { get; }

    /// <summary>The channel mode type classifications (A/B/C/D) from CHANMODES.</summary>
    ChannelModeTypes ChannelModes { get; }

    /// <summary>The prefix-to-mode mapping (e.g. @ → o, + → v) from PREFIX.</summary>
    PrefixMapping Prefix { get; }

    /// <summary>The maximum number of channels the bot can join, if advertised.</summary>
    int? MaxChannels { get; }

    /// <summary>The maximum nick length, if advertised.</summary>
    int? MaxNickLength { get; }

    /// <summary>The maximum topic length, if advertised.</summary>
    int? MaxTopicLength { get; }

    /// <summary>
    /// The maximum message length (default 512 minus CRLF and overhead).
    /// </summary>
    int MaxMessageLength { get; }

    /// <summary>The channel type prefixes supported by this server (e.g. '#', '&amp;').</summary>
    IReadOnlySet<char> ChannelTypes { get; }

    /// <summary>Returns whether the server advertises the specified ISUPPORT token.</summary>
    bool Supports(string token);

    /// <summary>Gets the value of the specified ISUPPORT token, or null if not present.</summary>
    string? GetValue(string token);
}

📝 Write ChannelModeTypes.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Platform/ChannelModeTypes.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Platform;

/// <summary>
/// Classifies channel modes into types A through D, as defined by the CHANMODES ISUPPORT token.
/// Mode types determine whether a mode takes a parameter when set/unset.
/// </summary>
public sealed class ChannelModeTypes
{
    /// <summary>Type A: list modes (e.g. ban lists). Always have a parameter.</summary>
    public IReadOnlySet<char> TypeA { get; }

    /// <summary>Type B: modes that always have a parameter (e.g. channel key).</summary>
    public IReadOnlySet<char> TypeB { get; }

    /// <summary>Type C: modes that have a parameter only when set (e.g. channel limit).</summary>
    public IReadOnlySet<char> TypeC { get; }

    /// <summary>Type D: modes that never have a parameter (e.g. no-external-messages).</summary>
    public IReadOnlySet<char> TypeD { get; }

    /// <summary>
    /// Creates a new <see cref="ChannelModeTypes"/> from the four mode categories.
    /// </summary>
    public ChannelModeTypes(
        IReadOnlySet<char> typeA,
        IReadOnlySet<char> typeB,
        IReadOnlySet<char> typeC,
        IReadOnlySet<char> typeD)
    {
        TypeA = typeA;
        TypeB = typeB;
        TypeC = typeC;
        TypeD = typeD;
    }

    /// <summary>
    /// Parses the CHANMODES ISUPPORT value (e.g. "beI,k,l,imnpst").
    /// </summary>
    public static ChannelModeTypes Parse(string chanmodesValue)
    {
        var parts = chanmodesValue.Split(',');
        return new ChannelModeTypes(
            parts.Length > 0 ? new HashSet<char>(parts[0]) : new HashSet<char>(),
            parts.Length > 1 ? new HashSet<char>(parts[1]) : new HashSet<char>(),
            parts.Length > 2 ? new HashSet<char>(parts[2]) : new HashSet<char>(),
            parts.Length > 3 ? new HashSet<char>(parts[3]) : new HashSet<char>()
        );
    }

    /// <summary>Default mode types for servers that don't advertise CHANMODES.</summary>
    public static ChannelModeTypes Default { get; } = Parse("beI,k,l,imnpst");
}

📝 Write PrefixMapping.cs
/workspaces/marv/src/Marv.Core/Platform/PrefixMapping.cs
namespace Marv.Core.Platform;

/// <summary>
/// Maps channel membership mode characters to their display prefixes,
/// as defined by the PREFIX ISUPPORT token (e.g. "(ov)@+").
/// </summary>
public sealed class PrefixMapping
{
    private readonly IReadOnlyList<(char Mode, char Prefix)> _entries;
    private readonly Dictionary<char, char> _modeToPrefix;
    private readonly Dictionary<char, char> _prefixToMode;

    /// <summary>
    /// Creates a new <see cref="PrefixMapping"/> from ordered mode/prefix pairs.
    /// Entries are ordered by descending privilege (highest first).
    /// </summary>
    public PrefixMapping(IReadOnlyList<(char Mode, char Prefix)> entries)
    {
        _entries = entries;
        _modeToPrefix = entries.ToDictionary(e => e.Mode, e => e.Prefix);
        _prefixToMode = entries.ToDictionary(e => e.Prefix, e => e.Mode);
    }

    /// <summary>All known prefix characters, ordered by descending privilege.</summary>
    public IEnumerable<char> Prefixes => _entries.Select(e => e.Prefix);

    /// <summary>All known mode characters, ordered by descending privilege.</summary>
    public IEnumerable<char> Modes => _entries.Select(e => e.Mode);

    /// <summary>Gets the display prefix for a mode character (e.g. 'o' → '@').</summary>
    public char? GetPrefix(char mode) => _modeToPrefix.TryGetValue(mode, out var p) ? p : null;

    /// <summary>Gets the mode character for a display prefix (e.g. '@' → 'o').</summary>
    public char? GetMode(char prefix) => _prefixToMode.TryGetValue(prefix, out var m) ? m : null;

    /// <summary>Returns whether the given character is a known prefix.</summary>
    public bool IsPrefix(char c) => _prefixToMode.ContainsKey(c);

    /// <summary>
    /// Parses the PREFIX ISUPPORT value (e.g. "(ov)@+").
    /// </summary>
    public static PrefixMapping Parse(string prefixValue)
    {
        if (string.IsNullOrEmpty(prefixValue) || prefixValue[0] != '(')
            return Default;

        var closeIndex = prefixValue.IndexOf(')');
        if (closeIndex < 0)
            return Default;

        var modes = prefixValue[1..closeIndex];
        var prefixes = prefixValue[(closeIndex + 1)..];

        if (modes.Length != prefixes.Length)
            return Default;

        var entries = new List<(char, char)>(modes.Length);
        for (var i = 0; i < modes.Length; i++)
            entries.Add((modes[i], prefixes[i]));

        return new PrefixMapping(entries);
    }

    /// <summary>Default prefix mapping for servers that don't advertise PREFIX.</summary>
    public static PrefixMapping Default { get; } = Parse("(ov)@+");
}

namespace Marv.Core.Platform;

/// <summary>
/// String constants for known IRCv3 capabilities, to avoid magic strings throughout the codebase.
/// </summary>
public static class Capabilities
{
    /// <summary>Allows receiving and sending arbitrary message tags.</summary>
    public const string MessageTags = "message-tags";

    /// <summary>Provides accurate timestamps on messages.</summary>
    public const string ServerTime = "server-time";

    /// <summary>Server echoes the bot's own PRIVMSG/NOTICE messages back.</summary>
    public const string EchoMessage = "echo-message";

    /// <summary>Every message carries the sender's services account name.</summary>
    public const string AccountTag = "account-tag";

    /// <summary>Correlates sent commands with server responses.</summary>
    public const string LabeledResponse = "labeled-response";

    /// <summary>Groups related messages together.</summary>
    public const string Batch = "batch";

    /// <summary>Server notifies when capabilities become available/unavailable.</summary>
    public const string CapNotify = "cap-notify";

    /// <summary>Secure authentication during connection registration.</summary>
    public const string Sasl = "sasl";

    /// <summary>Receive all prefix modes in NAMES/WHO responses.</summary>
    public const string MultiPrefix = "multi-prefix";

    /// <summary>JOIN messages include account name and realname.</summary>
    public const string ExtendedJoin = "extended-join";

    /// <summary>Notified when users change away status.</summary>
    public const string AwayNotify = "away-notify";

    /// <summary>Notified when users log in/out of services accounts.</summary>
    public const string AccountNotify = "account-notify";

    /// <summary>Notified when users are invited to channels.</summary>
    public const string InviteNotify = "invite-notify";

    /// <summary>Notified when a user's host/ident changes.</summary>
    public const string Chghost = "chghost";

    /// <summary>Notified when a user changes their realname.</summary>
    public const string Setname = "setname";

    /// <summary>NAMES replies include full nick!user@host masks.</summary>
    public const string UserhostInNames = "userhost-in-names";

    /// <summary>Marks the bot with a B flag so users and servers know it is a bot.</summary>
    public const string BotMode = "bot";

    /// <summary>Standardized FAIL/WARN/NOTE server responses.</summary>
    public const string StandardReplies = "standard-replies";

    /// <summary>Watch for specific nicks coming online/offline.</summary>
    public const string Monitor = "monitor";
}
